in writing. - i do not support the resolution on the draft agreement. the reason for this is that it speaks of a move towards economic and fiscal union. while agreements are needed to boost competitiveness in weak member states, economic and fiscal union goes further. the european parliament also calls for a roadmap for euro bonds. i can't support that either. it reduces the incentive for budgetary discipline, because countries in difficulty can still finance themselves at too low an interest rate. in greece, too low interest rates have contributed to the inflow of money which proved unrecoverable due to insufficient competitiveness. that's not why i'm here. it would also be very costly for those countries which, because of their sound budgetary discipline, are now borrowing at low interest rates. i am also in favour of the new agreement, which should help to strengthen budgetary discipline. a step in the right direction for two reasons: the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the budgetary discipline and the budgetary discipline of the participating countries, and will continue to monitor the implementation of the budgetary discipline.

deputising for the rapporteur. − mr president, this may be a surprise, but problems at schipol airport, where there was a bomb alert today, have meant that ms essayah unfortunately cannot be here at the moment, so she has asked me to represent her. on her behalf, i would like to say that she is very happy with the outcome of the trialogue and the agreement which we reached with the commission and the council. she would like to thank the representatives of the council, commissioner barnier, the chair of the econ committee, ms bowles, the shadow rapporteurs and all those who proposed amendments and contributed to this excellent result, as well as the econ secretariat which makes our work possible. the completion of the single european payment area is of great importance for the european internal market and has numerous benefits for our citizens. from february 2014 onwards, all electronic payments in the euro area will be as easy as cash payments. citizens and companies will benefit from sepa in several ways, but the most obvious of them is that is possible to make payments to all over europe from a single bank account. that is now possible for everyone. all cross-border debit and credit transfers can now be done as if they were normal domestic payments. with sepa only one bank account is needed for the whole euro area by using the international bank account number or iban. this has many great advantages: only one bank account will be needed for the whole euro area. if a person is working abroad he or she will not need a new bank account in that country and may receive his or her salary to the one bank account in his or her own home country. sepa will also make it possible to use one and the same debit card anywhere in the euro area, so when you are on holiday or visiting another euro area country you will be able to benefit from these possibilities. sepa is good news at present concerning the euro and the eu. it will open up national markets to competition and make the payment sector more effective. according to an impact study published by the commission, sepa will benefit the european economy by eur 123 billion over the next six years. the benefits will be even greater because sepa creates favourable conditions for innovations such as automatically combining payments with the book-keeping of a company. this is the case in finland and in other countries such as the netherlands, which started implementing sepa at a very early stage. easy payments also make it very easy to buy goods and services via the internet from all over the eu. consumers will have real options to compare prices and get the best price. buying a computer from the cheapest eu retailer may save hundreds of euros, making prices go down everywhere in the single market. therefore sepa is in many ways essential for a well-functioning internal market. since moving to sepa has been too slow in the past, this regulation sets a clear end-date for the transition. parliament has succeeded in negotiating a single end date for both sepa credit transfers and direct debits: 1 february 2014. in those member states which do not have the euro as their currency, the date will be 30 october 2016. this will make it easier to adapt to the change and to carry out information campaigns for consumers. parliament has also achieved the right level of consumer protection: consumers may now instruct their banks to limit direct debit collection to certain amounts and to establish positive or negative lists of payees. it will no longer be necessary for consumers to provide banks with special bic codes for domestic or cross-border payments after the end-date and after two years. for ease of adaptation to the new system, several waivers provide a two-year transition period after the end-date. for example, a member state may allow companies which use badge files sent to their payment service providers to avoid using the standard message format xml for two years. a member state may also allow consumers to use their old bank account number instead of ibans for two extra years. the payment services directive is being reviewed later this year; this will be an important step in finalising the entire system and ensuring that it works. on behalf of ms essayah, i would like to underline that we are very happy for both citizens and companies in the european union and throughout the sepa area that all cross-border transfers and direct debits can be done as if they were normal domestic payments. we have now managed to achieve a real single european payments area.

rapporteur. − mr president, if there is one lesson that we could learn from the past few years it is that our economies are totally intertwined. yes, we do need to coordinate. yes, we should let the commission look over our shoulders. this coordination adds necessary powers at the european level but, unfortunately, without adding necessary democracy at the european level too. we as a parliament do not accept such a limited role. with my report and the report of mr gauzès, we are presenting parliament’s view on what is currently most needed. although the council is not legally obliged to take account of it, i feel that it most certainly is morally and democratically obliged to do so. parliament’s main message to the council is this: we need far more coherence between fiscal and budgetary policies on the one hand and social and employment policies on the other hand. it is a tremendous mistake to ignore the social consequences of badly-aimed austerity policies or to forget that a currency is there to serve the people, not the other way round. i really hope that, at the spring council, one government leader will stand up and say: ‘dear colleagues, just hold on a minute. do you remember we had a meeting about two years ago where we set our goals for 2020 and, among other things, planned to increase labour participation, lift millions out of poverty and invest heavily in innovation? i am getting quite worried that, if we go on like this, we will not attain any of these goals. ‘ correct me if i am wrong, but if we all cut social benefits to get under the deficit ceiling, will that not actually push rather a lot of people into poverty instead of lifting millions out? if we cut down on things like labour activation measures and childcare, will that not really mean that labour participation will diminish instead of grow and that rising unemployment will, in turn, increase our budget deficits? ‘ furthermore, of course my calculator could be at fault, but is it not the case that if countries like greece, spain and portugal are not given any hope for recovery their debt will rise even as they are paying it off, because it is a percentage of their gdp and not actually a flat figure? ‘ perhaps what we should be doing is discussing how we can cut wisely in some areas and also invest in others. perhaps we should look further to the future and prepare ourselves for an ageing society by investing in education, innovation and youth employment. perhaps we should make our eu 2020 goals binding: as binding as the budgetary ones. ‘ i know that i am probably complicating things quite a bit by pointing this out, but as i was reading the european parliament’s contribution to our discussion i thought, by golly, they are absolutely right.’ well, any government leader who is courageous enough to point out the pain is very welcome to borrow that speech, and i am very sure that tomorrow’s vote will show that any such courageous leader would have the full backing of the european parliament.
rapporteur. − mr president, i would like to thank all of you for this debate and to thank the shadows for working closely in a very speedy process. special thanks go to mr gauzès and mrs berès for the excellent cooperation on our reports. i am very proud to form a troika together with you, to refer to what mrs rapti said. we have shown that it is possible to deliver complementary views from the economic and the social side, and i am happy with the broad support expressed in this house for our work. i agree, mrs harkin, that both deficit and surplus countries should be addressed. unfortunately, in yesterday’s alert mechanism report, surplus countries like the netherlands and germany were unaffected. i hope mrs harkin will be able to support the paragraph in my report that counters this asymmetrical approach. i am happy that the call for a job-rich recovery, expressing worries about rising poverty, is growing louder in all institutions and all political groups. what i am afraid of now is that this call will remain lip service. we need measures to give the employment and social goals the power and standing to rise to the level of the budgetary ones, so perhaps we need an excessive unemployment procedure and an excessive poverty-increase procedure. why not expand what was once the six-pack – and is now going to be the eight-pack – immediately to a ten-pack, in order also to include the social and employment goals. and let us differentiate our approach, to give all member states a prospect of recovery.

author. − mr president, the issue at stake here today is whether we can be certain that our own european laws always apply within europe, or whether they could be overruled by third country laws. the us, for example, considers that its jurisdiction does not only extend to activities and actors within the us, but also the activities of such actors elsewhere in the world. with that interpretation, us law has extra-territorial effect within europe. the us, for example, can subpoena companies with a presence in the us to provide data stored in europe. we have identified, in our oral questions, a number of laws that might have this effect – for example the patriot act, the medicare act on medical data and insurance data, the foreign intelligence and surveillance act, and the foreign account tax compliance act (fatca). however, data stored in europe is covered by eu law and companies there have to comply with eu law. the commission acknowledges this, but what action has actually been taken to protect companies that find themselves caught between conflicting jurisdictions? this problem does not only concern the extra-territorial effect from us law, but potentially from any other country. there are also other countries where european companies have a presence. for example, more and more companies have operations in china, and they too can be confronted with this problem. as a matter of fact, we are getting more confirmation nowadays that such companies are being confronted with this problem. this does not only concern eu data protection laws, as the title of today’s debate suggests, but all kinds of laws. fatca, notably, raise strong concerns for european banks and insurers. commissioner, we hope to discuss this matter soon in the committee on economic and monetary affairs with your colleague, commissioner barnier. i would urge you to act now. we do not need new legislation, but it is key that we enforce existing european legislation in the interests of our citizens and our companies.
author. − mr president, i do not know about the procedure. simply, the most important question has remained unanswered, and i would like to be able to put the question again to the commissioner in order to get an answer before we leave. the question is: under current eu data protection rules, what can a company do to protect itself if it has an operation in the us and it has to comply with the subpoena? the subpoena says that data stored in europe – and therefore covered by eu law – must be provided to the us authorities. this is not through a mutual legal assistance treaty; this is a company which has an operation in the us and is being obliged to provide data stored in the eu. that data is covered by eu laws. how is the commission going to protect those companies, which are asking for protection?
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0176/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0176/97). partners who want to be together, parents and children who want to be together and who now, for whatever reason, live in different countries. but it seems that member states are using that directive for the opposite, to deter migrants, to deter what they call in my country, the netherlands, 'disadvantaged migrants'. this is a flagrant abuse of a directive which is intended to respect family life and thus also to respect human rights. countries are ageing: can you get married at 18 or can you wait until you're 21? countries set income requirements for migrants who want to do family reunification, income requirements that they don't set for residents. countries are increasingly setting examination requirements: they require you to speak the language before you enter a country and they won't give you a residence permit if you don't speak the language. i see that in the netherlands. i see people trying to bring their partner to the netherlands, but if the partner happens to be illiterate, never learned to read and write, then he or she will not simply pass the dutch citizenship exam. the dutch authorities are thus saying: it will, with your right to family reunification. i therefore ask the european commission for clarification. can the european commission do this or should it introduce stricter rules to combat these abuses? after all, this is a question of family reunification and not - as i hear my french colleague say - of the absorption capacity of a country. it's not about that. it's about the right to be together. so could we be doing more to change this kind of abuse of integration legislation, or is that really very unwise? if we open pandora's box, are we gonna get a lot of trouble? the netherlands seems to be setting a trend. belgium is also now using integration rules to refuse permits. france seems to find it interesting. the question therefore is to us, to this parliament and to the european commission: we are not going to let the matter go on as it is and try to get judgments through the courts which are binding on europe to prevent abuse. and are we trying to use the directive for what it is intended to do, namely family migration, or are we trying to adapt the directive so that abuse no longer occurs? i have to be honest with you, i would like to see improvements, i would like to see tightening up, but i think our political line should be this: we are keeping it as it is and we are encouraging citizens to go to court and to claim their right to bring their partner to europe.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97). this isn't about immigration policy. as claude moraes said, of course, you can't separate the two completely, but let's keep a close eye on what this is all about. this is about immigrants who want to build a stable family life. that is precisely what is in favour of integration. people who are forced to remain alone while their loved ones and their families live elsewhere have no stable situation. it is therefore in our interest to ensure that people can live in a stable family situation. we should also bear in mind that there are still many people who cannot claim this right, for example people who live together unmarried with children or same-sex couples. i therefore welcome the extremely forward-looking opinion of the competent committee in the council of europe to the european commission in this regard. of course, abuse must be prevented and forced marriages are something i abhor. but i must say, ladies and gentlemen - and this is a purely personal comment - that i think it is a bit of casual feminism to say that we must now fight this with this instrument. if we're going to empower women, i can think of a few things, frankly. we must focus on better implementation of the directive, on reducing the number of different interpretations, on bringing about more harmonization, a more harmonized application of the directive, rather than increasing the differences. i am therefore pleased to note from the commissioner's words that there is no support for the restrictions requested by the dutch government.
president. - i asked the dutch minister for immigration and asylum, mr leers, recently whether in his crusade against family reunification he had no compassion for people who love each other. his answer was: "life happiness is not a criterion for me". how can you say that? i am not a christian democrat! didn't christian democrats once say that the family is the cornerstone of society? does not christianity teach that love is the highest good? and does not the human rights convention state that family life needs protection? not according to this minister, who thereby denies his parentage and party. i now receive many emails from people who are desperate, who cannot build a family life because of the current dutch policy of integration tests, increased income requirements and far too high costs for residence permits. that's true for everyone, but especially for lgbt people. lawyers are now advising them to take the european route. that is to say, they move to another european member state because no member state has a policy as hardline as the netherlands. how far can a government sink? fortunately, commissioner malmström has shown time and again that her happiness does matter. abuse must be prevented, but europe will not make it impossible for lovers to live together in one of the member states. i'm still struggling with the green book. does the commission not thereby send the signal that family reunification policy is under discussion? could that energy not be better spent punishing a minister who is a disgrace to christian democracy and to the community of values that europe claims to be?
mr president, the influx of family immigrants into the netherlands will again be over 40 000 this year. family reunification is thus one of the main drivers behind this ongoing mass immigration, with problems such as cultural alienation, increasing ecological footprint and threatening the welfare state. i would stress that this is a matter of family immigration. they are not refugees in danger or at risk in their own country. the netherlands is now asking for the european directive to be extended so that we can decide on migration ourselves. after all, each member state is different. the netherlands wants to have room for its own necessary policies, but commissioner malmström believes that the problems of one country do not count. is she in this for the europeans? no, commissioner malmström's green paper is for immigrants. the commission argues that whole families should settle in the eu, but the right to family life can be exercised just as well, if not better, in the country of origin. commissioner malmström has no regard for the reasonable requirement that member states should be able to decide for themselves on matters affecting their survival. i would like to ask the commission once again to put the interests of the member states and the interests of society first. regulations must serve the interests of all, not just the interests of immigrants and the commission in its ivory tower. the speaker is prepared to answer a 'blue card' question (rule 149 (8))
"blue card" question. what is it? president, mr zijlstra, i hear you say that it should be the dutch government itself which decides whether or not to admit family migrants. that's actually the case. my question to you is: are you aware that most family migration in the netherlands involves people coming to someone who has a dutch passport? whether that is someone who was born and raised in the netherlands or someone who later got a dutch passport, that is another matter, but it is dutch people with a dutch passport who bring a migrant, their partner or their child, to the netherlands. the netherlands could arrange that themselves, but they do not. why not, mr zijlstra?
mr sargentini wonders why the netherlands does not apply the rules that apparently exist. my answer is: there are no rules. the requirements the netherlands wants to impose on partner migration relate, for example, to a minimum income and to matters such as when there may be an independent right of residence. the european parliament considers that the european directive is still too restrictive in these matters. the netherlands is therefore not asking for the directive to be applied more strictly throughout europe, but for the netherlands to be given the possibility of applying wider rules.
the report on the implementation of the family migration directive and the green paper identify problems and shortcomings in national implementation. however, what some member states, including the netherlands, are doing is turning the problem around. they claim that the directive leaves room for abuse. does the commission have data or have member states provided data to support these concerns? the eu needs a common immigration policy. yes, but it's about the most basic rights, that's what it's about, too, like the right to a private and family life and the right to marry and have a family. the same applies to refugees, who are now being questioned by the green paper as to whether they should be included. is the commission aware of the practice in the netherlands of family reunification of refugees who have acquired status, where the criterion of actual family situation is used? is the commission aware that establishing such a de facto family relationship requires not only that there is, for example, a biological link, but also that interviews are conducted, including with very young children, and that mothers are told on the basis of so-called wrong answers that they have no relationship with their own child? what is the commission going to do about this? the netherlands is fighting for basic rights and wants to return to agreements made. are we letting member states get away with going back to international conventions on fundamental rights? i want to hear no. i want to hear from the commission that they are going to ensure that we respect and uphold rights in europe and not agree to these hardline proposals.

rapporteur. − madam president, my report, which is an own-initiative report, addresses a number of issues, some of which are controversial, sometimes surprisingly controversial. i will start with the first one: the one on quotas or binding targets for more women on the executive boards of listed companies. i would like to start with a quote by an american feminist who said, decades ago already: true equality will only have been achieved if we have as many incompetent women in positions of power as incompetent men. i do not think i need to say much more about quotas, but i do not think there is anybody who is really in favour of quotas. the trouble is that, so far, voluntary measures have achieved next to nothing, and we need to do something because doing nothing is not an option. if we see the report last week from the commission demonstrating that only 13 % of board members are female and only 3 % are actually leading listed companies, that is pathetic. the commission promised us concrete measures last year and last week the report, as i said, exposed the complete lack of progress. i have to say to the commissioner that i am slightly disappointed at the follow-up. you propose another consultation, commissioner, whereas after decades of stagnation and after a year of trying with voluntary measures, i would say that the time to act is here; it is no longer a time for words. another issue that to my surprise turned out to be slightly controversial and something that i would like to put on the political agenda is that of single-person households, not to be confused with single-parent households – single-person households. thirty per cent of all households, europe-wide, are single-person households. only one person – not with children, just one person – and they are in relative terms and in absolute terms placed at a disadvantage; a huge disadvantage, compared to others when it comes to taxation, social security, housing, pensions, insurance and what have you. the european union does have all sorts of policies in support of families, which is fine; i support that. but demographic developments show that the composition of households is changing and it is time that the commission recognised the new demographic reality and ensured fair and equal treatment for people living alone. no one should be either rewarded or penalised for the size or composition of their household. i am also very happy that parliament wholeheartedly endorsed the paragraph calling, or rather reiterating, its call, for a road map on lgbt rights. commissioner, this is a very strong political appeal by this house – a repeated appeal – to the commission to come forward with proposals for a road map analogous to the gender equality road map. if we can do it for women, and the european union has achieved a lot for women and gender equality, we can do it for lgbt people. finally, i am also surprised and a little bit dismayed at amendments against the unblocking of the horizontal anti-discrimination directive and the calls to give fresh impetus to the talks on maternity and paternity leave. i mean, if you do not want quotas, if you do not want to address the gender pay gap, if you do not want to unblock the horizontal anti-discrimination directive, if you do not want to talk about maternity and paternity leave, then what do people actually want to do for gender equality? because it is not going to happen spontaneously, colleagues; it is not going to happen. we have been waiting for decades; now is the time to act.

rapporteur. − madam president, i thank the commissioner for her strong words. i think you can count on the majority of this house to support any measures you take. first of all briefly on quotas: i think there are a couple of misunderstandings here. quotas, as many people have said, are indeed a necessary evil. this is not an instrument to push incompetent women into top jobs. this is an instrument to break the glass ceiling, which is not a visible brick wall but consists of very subtle, invisible, sometimes even subconscious mechanisms that favour men over women. that is what we need to tackle. secondly, i heard the word ‘subsidiarity’ a number of times in the debate, when we talked about lgbt families in particular. frankly, i think that the subsidiarity principle is invoked rather selectively. we very often discuss matters here which are outside our competence but i would say that free movement is one of the four pillars of a europe without borders. that is definitely the competence of the commission, as are equality policies. so why is addressing the recognition of lgbt families not an eu competence, when we have an eu alliance for families which is funded or co-funded by the commission? why does that only apply to one single model of families? why not to all families in the whole of the european union, even in those countries where the families exist but the people are not recognised? in my country for example, same-sex couples can legally marry. it is a law and i find it very annoying that a law in my country is not recognised in another country. this is not about imposing gay marriage on all member states. it is simply about mutual recognition. we recognise the ingredients of cheese and beer across the european union. why do we not recognise love and family, the most important things in our lives?
the turning point seems to have been reached. the markets are calming down and confidence in the eurozone seems to be recovering, as president van rompuy rightly said here. we are therefore going to meet an important basic condition for getting back on the road to economic growth and also for creating a confidence-building investment climate. let's face it, corporations have a lot of money in the bank. private investors are afraid to invest, and if confidence is restored, that money can be reinvested to fuel further economic growth. but everything will depend on whether we actually add butter to the fish. president van rompuy spoke about the growth strategy we need. but we have a europe 2020 strategy. but we must do it too. and the only way to do that is to put our hand in our own bosom. it does not help if the commission points to the council, the council to parliament and parliament to the member states. i think the excellent letter from the 12 heads of government, which points to the european commission and to what the european commission should do, is an excellent example of this. but if those government leaders make an effort of their own to combat tax evasion, to implement the europe 2020 strategy in their own budget. that means saving education by cutting back. but it does, for example, promote efficiency in public services. do what we promised and we'll get out of this crisis.
the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the application of the principle of subsidiarity. that is the amount these countries have received from the globalisation fund in recent years. and how sad that it is precisely these countries, countries that are doing relatively well, that are restricting access to the globalisation fund. when the crisis hit, the globalisation fund was extended to include large companies and industries that had gone bankrupt due to the crisis, resulting in thousands of redundancies. and if only it were true that this would no longer be necessary, that the crisis was already behind us and that there would no longer be mass redundancies due to crisis bankruptcies. but the opposite is true. the crisis is becoming more and more acute in all countries in these times of austerity. the crisis is the reason for the mass layoffs. i think it is cruel and morally unacceptable that countries like the netherlands, germany and sweden block the crisis derogation. and also incomprehensible. because these countries are against it because they are against the fund in general. the egf should not exist, and so we are against everything, but we are of course making good use of it in the meantime. because, as mrs harkin said, the netherlands has just barely managed to raise 8 million euros in 2011. the mature reasoning should of course be that as long as this fund exists it should be used as usefully as possible. the crisis is a very useful investment.

the problems of piracy have only increased in recent years. what we see is more ships being hijacked, more seamen being taken hostage and the pirates' area of operation has become much larger. also, the price paid in lives and ransoms has increased. hundreds of seafarers are currently being held in camps in somalia, under conditions that you and i would never want to be in. it is therefore high time that a stronger approach was taken to piracy. and i think there are four points. the member states must really use all their resources to free the seamen who have been captured, and i believe that this includes targeted action on the somali coast. the second. the eu member states must all agree to the use of certified and armed security guards on board. now you see that one country does allow armed security guards, and the other country does not. but those pirates know exactly how those ships sail and they follow them exactly, so you know which ships have no security on board. so that's an extra risk, especially for those ships. so it's a security risk, but it's also a competition, it's competition. the third. there is evidence that the pirate leaders are laundering money through banks in africa and in europe. this means that the security services must identify these money flows and ensure that the banks that cooperate with them are dealt with. 4. other somalia is a lawless and lawless country. the commission must develop a plan to improve somalia. as far as i am concerned, commissioner piebalgs, there can be a little more ambition, and i would like to hear your answers.

on behalf of the alde group when we talk about water and its great international importance, we often refer to a famous quote from the world bank's director in the 1990s. and that is as follows: if the wars of this century were fought over oil, the wars of the next century will be fought over water. a very ominous prediction indeed. we're gonna be beating each other's brains out about water shortages. but the quote goes on, and often that's forgotten. the sequel is: unless we change our approach to managing this precious and vital resource. because unlike oil, we are talking about a renewable resource that we can use as often as we want. only then we have to deal with it in a very sensible way. this is the subject of the world water forum in marseille, and it is also the subject of the rio+20 conference in june. this week the united nations world water development report was released. that report is also alarming, but it is not defeatist. there are some very critical elements in it, and the depletion of groundwater is a problem that is completely underestimated. in china, in the middle east, in california, and in many other parts of the world. and we must pay very close attention to this, and especially to the role of agriculture in this. but cooperation is of the utmost importance. yesterday the danish presidency concluded an agreement with china to export more technology from europe to china. china is going to invest 500 billion euros in water management over the next decade. and that might be a nice water-oil analogy, too. the water shortage is not only a threat, but also, of course, a very great economic opportunity. let us agree on this at rio+20 and make sure that we really achieve much better management of water internationally.

author. - refugees from the horn of africa seeking safety often travel via egypt to israel. they must then pass through the sinai desert. that desert is a no man's land that the egyptian government seems to have no control over. the journey through the desert is facilitated by bedouin smugglers who exploit the vulnerability of these refugees. women are raped, people are locked up for months to extort them further, people are killed for their organs or people are killed just because they can. this resolution takes the example of a young eritrean man, solomon. but there are many more solomons and myriams who are or were in the same circumstances in the sinai desert. and i think it is a fact that many female refugees, as soon as they know they are in israel, ask for an abortion. conceived in the desert. egypt is not doing enough to control the sinai and to prosecute the gangs and smugglers. egypt also still has no functioning asylum policy, which raises the suspicion that they would rather lose refugees than be rich, and that their well-being is not in egypt's interest. the european union is trying to help democracy in egypt to move forward after the arab spring. in a democracy, this is not the way to deal with refugees, and europe should make recognition of the geneva conventions a condition. but it is not only egypt that is guilty of abusing refugees. israel is sending people back into the desert. israel is now building the world's largest prison for refugees, and you don't lock up refugees. you've fled sudan, eritrea, somalia, and what's happening to you? you end up in egypt or israel, in a situation that is inhuman. i call on mrs ashton to take this to egypt and israel and to protest strongly.
author. − madam president, the sponsoring of the word economic forum in davos this year by the government of bahrain shows its ambition to belong and to be taken seriously by the international and business communities. bahrain’s authorities can only be credible if they respect its citizens’ human rights, period. in a country that heavily restricts freedom of speech and of the internet and has people fired for expressing their political opinions, business development is impossible. when tens of thousands of peaceful protestors are met with violence and doctors face political trials, investment is unattractive. where tensions between shia and sunnis are growing and citizens face military trials, there is a risk of a proxy conflict involving saudi arabia and iran. eu-based companies in bahrain should take back fired employees, and we should freeze the export of weapons and technology from the eu that is used to repress people. bahrain’s authorities should put themselves on the right side of history and show their unconditional commitment to respecting human rights and make reforms. the release of political prisoners, especially of abdulhadi alkhawaja and mahdi abu deeb, are of particular urgency to this house.

i think that wage dumping is the central theme of the question and i have a question about that too. in international road transport there are many drivers in the netherlands who are writing me dramatic letters at the moment saying that dutch companies are opening a branch in, say, romania, then taking the staff from there - with the same trucks they had in their company in the netherlands before - and then telling their dutch drivers that there is no more work for them unless they accept the working conditions that apply to the romanians. i think that is a direct form of wage dumping. a simple movement in europe can suddenly bring down working conditions for the same company. i do not see any guarantee in the commission's proposal for a directive on the posting of workers that this kind of fraudulent activity by companies will be prevented. i would like to know from the commission how we are doing this and whether it is europe's intention that we should reduce wages.

mr president, i have spent the last half hour enthusiastically retweeting mr andor’s tweets about the employment package – while of course listening very attentively to the rest of you – because i am really happy with the questions that the package raises. finally the debate seems to be shifting to the real economy and to the actual people who live in europe. finally there is attention to job creation and to labour demands and to greening the economy, which i think is a highly welcome and long overdue shift of the debate. the specific questions i have concern wages. firstly, will the commission adapt the scoreboard for macroeconomic imbalances in such a way that excessive wage moderation is considered an imbalance as well? secondly, will the commission address country-specific recommendations to those countries without decent minimum wages? thirdly, how do you envision making a tripartite forum for monitoring wage developments at eu level fruitful and durable? past attempts have not been that successful.
i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that ikea is a dutch company, as is eni, the italian oil company, coca cola hbc, the so-called greek soft drink bottler, and the largest portuguese supermarket, jerónimo martins. of course, these companies are not really dutch. this is an ingenious structure with an administrative office in the netherlands and real activities elsewhere. these companies do this in order to pay as little tax as possible through secret agreements with the dutch tax authorities. they often make use of the network of tax treaties established by the netherlands, which allows them to shift their profits to tax havens after paying a minimum tax in the netherlands. it is shocking to see such multinationals avoiding taxation, while people all over europe are having to tighten their belts because of the austerity rage. the heaviest shoulders carry the lightest loads. this problem cannot be solved at national level; the european union must act. in the resolution we call for transparency. the annual accounts of multinationals must be much clearer and more specific, i.e. they must give an insight into turnover and profit figures by country. tax treaties, both between member states and with third countries, should be scrutinised and coordinated to prevent their being misused for tax evasion. we must also have a european minimum rate of corporation tax. i hope the commission will take up this widely-received resolution and make proposals in a hurry.
it is always nice to see that when we talk in general terms about our tax system, we are always in favour of greening the tax system. we always want to include environmental costs in the pricing until it becomes concrete. then, according to some of our colleagues, all we have to do is to defend the single market. if so, please stop using that environmental rhetoric which is apparently only for the bühne. then we know for sure from now on that we will never have to hear that again. but let us use the facts well. they say the price of diesel is going up for everyone. that's just not true! perhaps for luxembourg, which now has a levy of 32 cents per litre. yes, that levy should go to 40 cents in 2018! indeed, luxembourg will suffer, but germany already has a 47-cent levy. so germany will not have to change anything until 2018. why can't i hear that? and the answer that, this could mean the end of the diesel engine, what nonsense! where's the most expensive gasoline? in the netherlands! 70% more expensive than diesel. where do you have the least diesel cars? right, in the netherlands! so let us use the facts, this is a very modest proposal and let us turn the rhetoric of environmental costs into action for once.
rapporteur. − mr president, today we are at the conclusion of a process which started in march 2003. we have been working on this transatlantic data sharing deal for nine years, and i think that – whatever the outcome of today’s vote – one conclusion that we can already draw is that it is telling and disappointing that, after nine years of negotiations with our closest friend and ally, we can only come up with an agreement that commands half-hearted support from a divided house. i therefore think that, whatever today’s outcome, we need to have a closer look at how we deal with our transatlantic friends and at the position of the european union in the transatlantic partnership. we need to reflect on whether we are not weak compared with our partner. as you all know, the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs (libe) voted a couple of weeks ago and – to my regret – rejected my recommendation to vote against the eu-us agreement, so the position of the libe committee is to recommend adoption of the agreement. however, i think that everybody – or almost everybody, including those who will vote in favour today – still has a number of questions. firstly, i would like to say something about a new element, namely the declaration of the european commission, which we received yesterday and which, as i see it, does not change anything: it does not contain any new elements. it is only the european commission’s interpretation and is not binding on the us, so it does not make us change our minds. with regard to the gue resolution proposing to submit this agreement to the european court of justice: although i have great sympathy for it and it is very tempting, i am a democrat and i am very reluctant to outsource political decision-making to the courts, and i think that this house should take its own responsibility. i said there are still a couple of questions that i would like to put to the commission. the commission has its own interpretation of article 4 on the purpose limitation, but i am still curious to find out – and i am sure many colleagues here would like to know – how you conclude that systematic use of the data for other purposes is explicitly excluded. which words in article 4 lead you to that conclusion? how do you conclude that the use of pnr data for public health purposes, immigration policies and customs is explicitly excluded, and how is the use of the data for those purposes based on articles 82 and 87 on police and justice cooperation? i think that much has been said about the retention periods. i would also like to ask the commission about push and pull. you say that pull will only be used in a very limited number of exceptional cases, but i would like to put some figures before you that we received from the association of european airlines. just a few figures from 2010 and 2011 show that there are ad hoc pulls tens of thousands of times a day. in december 2010, for example, there were 82 500 individual ad hoc pulls a day. i would like to know: how you can conclude that that is exceptional? concerning judicial redress: the agreement specifically states that no rights may be derived from this agreement, either for individuals or for other legal persons. how can you come to the conclusion that there is full judicial redress for eu citizens? i realise my time is up and i would like to save the rest of my time for after the debate.
blue-card question. − mr president, i have a question for mr voss and his political group. he rightly points out that there needs to be a legal base for the transfer of passenger data, so i wonder why this house has not reacted to the fact that, for three and a half years now, data has been transferred to canada in the absence of any legal base and that, under the us secure flight programme, data is being transferred from europe to the united states for flights not going to the united states, while this house does not seem to care. why is it that we want a legal base in one case and not in the other cases?
blue-card question. – mr president, i have two brief questions for mr kirkhope. you said we have to accept the authority of the legal department of the european commission. i ask you: do you accept the authority of the european data protection supervisor and the article 29 working group, who have both pointed out that this agreement is a violation of eu law? my second question: you rightfully referred to security purposes, but i would like to know why the conservatives – who are always concerned about small government and stating their preference for it over big government – allow the systematic use of these data for public health purposes, immigration policies and customs policies?
the primary task of the public authorities is security, and with this in mind, in europe each member state has the responsibility to protect its own citizens against terrorism. never should any country be prevented from doing so by brussels' meddling. the citizens have a right to expect security. countries should therefore decide for themselves who may enter their territory and what conditions are attached to this. and that applies, of course, to countries outside europe. america knows perfectly well how to fight terrorism, so what is brussels doing? if a country demands personal data from anyone who wants to enter its territory for security reasons, it is not only its right, it is its duty. if a citizen does not want to give up his personal data, he will not be able to enter. that is a personal decision, with which brussels has nothing to do. because, mr president, let me be clear, personal data is the property of the individual, not of companies and certainly not of brussels! it is therefore absolutely not for brussels to negotiate whether or not to provide personal data of eu citizens to third parties. president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs and security, on the situation in the middle east. but this agreement does not even acknowledge that islam is one of the greatest inspirations for modern terrorism, and that is shameful. brussels is naive and the member states accept the limitation of their responsibility towards their own citizens. and i blame them for that. "blue card" is the answer. president. - this is an example of the naivety i mentioned earlier. i believe that i can say from the figures that there were 11 000 attacks in muslim countries last year, all of them terrorism inspired by islam. where do you think the attacks in toulouse, in madrid, in london, the twin towers came from? how can i prove that islam is a source of terrorism? look around you and read the paper. and again: it is a shame that the only members of parliament who seem to recognize this are on this side of the house. i heard mrs ernst say that security is an illusion. the attitude of this parliament is indeed that.
the example of toulouse was mentioned earlier today, and i would like to mention the example of the nigerian who flew from yemen via amsterdam to detroit. his father had called the american embassy and said: "my son is up to no good". the french authorities also had information about the terrorist in toulouse. our security services have information about individuals who have evil intentions and they don't do the right thing with it. i think it would be primarily to our own security, if our security services did what they could with the information they have. on the other hand, pnr is actually a form of signals policy. in times of danger, people do strange things, people make irrational decisions, and then you can't go back. collecting data from every traveller around the world who moves is more about signals politics than effective counterterrorism.

rapporteur. − madam president, very quickly, first of all, i would like to clear up a misunderstanding. i have said this countless times: nobody in this house disputes the need to use data in the fight against terrorism and serious transnational crime, mrs foster – nobody. but we do dispute the use of these data for other purposes like communicable diseases, as the commission said, and immigration and customs checks. secondly, i would like to put a question to the commission. you have a certain interpretation of article 4 that is not shared by everybody, but what is key here is this: is it shared by the united states authorities and how do we know? thirdly, i would like to address mrs corazza bildt. yes, politics is about compromises, but certain things are not negotiable, like fundamental rights and eu law. i do not understand how we are able to reach a good agreement or an acceptable agreement with our australian friends and not with our american friends and allies. that escapes me completely. i think the analysis by our epp colleague, mr engel, was spot on. without the agreement, we do not have weaker safeguards, because the safeguards that are there are provided by existing us legislation and will still be there, even if we vote down this agreement. on push and pull, commissioner, as renate weber right said, not all carriers will shift to ‘pull’, and you know and we know that this clause is not enforceable. even if ‘push’ is in place, as it is today, ‘pull’ is still being used. this means that the americans go into our computer systems and pull the data. i have not heard you answer my question: why do you qualify as ‘exceptional’ tens of thousands of pulls – up to 82 000 – a day? how, frankly, do you qualify that as exceptional? finally, on monitoring. if the experience with the swift agreement and the monitoring of that is anything to go by, monitoring is a joke. one last question. colleagues, ask yourselves, look yourselves in the eye and ask yourselves: if other countries knock on our door and ask – and we know that they will, as qatar and japan have already done, and south korea and cuba will come, south africa will come, china and russia too – are we willing to give them our data for use in profiling on the same terms as we are doing for the united states now? that is a key question.
for the greens/efa group the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of victims of trafficking in human beings. in the debates we have had in this parliament, the greens have tried to bring forward generous proposals, giving protection to victims of trafficking in the form of a residence permit. we have not succeeded in doing so, it has become a temporary residence permit and a temporary residence permit means that after, for example, a court case, you could be sent back to the place from which you were trafficked, perhaps also to the people who helped to traffick you. that's still a shortcoming. i am glad to see that the victims package now being negotiated with the council recognizes that victims deserve support regardless of their legal status, i.e. whether they are legal or illegal. that will help victims of human trafficking.
the directive on trafficking in human beings, mr president, provides greater protection for the victims and better means of dealing with the perpetrators. unfortunately, however, the member states are still not making full use of the opportunities offered by the directive and, moreover, are still not tackling prostitution in particular. we know that 80% of the women and girls trafficked end up in prostitution, mainly through a mixture of lies, false promises and corruption. and then they're trapped in that underworld. we must try to get them out of it, madam president, because unfortunately this crime is still not widely recognised. the result is that european projects to warn or to take care of women and girls are barely getting off the ground. the cooperation between the police, particularly between western and eastern europe, really needs to be much better. we must not hide this crime out of shame, but we must make use of the directive. i call on the member states to do so and i support the european commissioner in this. we must get rid of these crimes, because they can still happen too much unhindered and we must continue to fight against them.

in writing. - i do not support the lulling report. the principle of reforming the taxation of energy products and electricity on the basis of co2 emissions and energy content is positive. however, higher energy taxes should be compensated in other areas, for example by lower taxes on labour. the eu does not (rightly) provide for this. in order to make a successful revision of the energy tax, compensation with other taxes at national level is necessary. as it is not currently provided for, the consequences of the present proposal are insufficient to assess.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, commissioner, a lot has been said already here about the quality of the fingerprints and the whole system and different steps in the chain, but i would like to raise another issue, and that is the justification for having biometrics in passports in the first place. in the netherlands, a recent request for access to documents revealed that the justification given at the time, namely document fraud and particularly lookalike fraud, was actually only established in between 20 and 40 cases in the course of a year in a country with nearly 17 million inhabitants. so one wonders why this whole system – which is extremely costly and which clearly has a lot of shortcomings – had to be introduced on such a thin justification. i wonder if the commission is going to include that element and that information in its evaluation.

member of the commission. − mr president, on behalf of the commission and in particular of my colleague connie hedegaard, who unfortunately cannot be here today, i very much welcome the report on women and climate change. i would like to thank the rapporteur for her excellent work and also for her very convincing opening statement. this is a good opportunity to discuss how the eu can help women put their stamp on future climate policy. climate change is already a fact of life for many on our planet, especially in the developing world. as you know, climate-change effects hit the most vulnerable the hardest. in the poorest and least-developed countries, women, children and the elderly are the first to suffer. as the report rightly says, even though climate change is gender-neutral, its effects are gender-differentiated. when addressing climate change, the eu looks at how consumption and lifestyle patterns have an impact on our environment. by supporting sustainable, green, low-emission development, we are helping to mitigate the effects of climate change and to stimulate economic development, which will make the poor and vulnerable – predominantly women and children – the principal beneficiaries. the eu supports mitigation actions through the geographic and thematic programmes of this development cooperation. as an example, i would like to mention the sustainable energy for all summit this week in brussels, where two panels were dedicated to access to sustainable energy for women. better access to modern sustainable energy – to cooking stoves, for example – will reduce the use of traditional biomass, which will in turn reduce deforestation. it will also increase the available time women have, which would otherwise be spent on collecting wood and cooking on inefficient fires. we have to ensure that we have more of such win-win programmes. the eu also aims to mainstream climate-change adaptation into eu policies including those on migration and disaster risk reduction. it is all about being forward-looking in order to minimise the inevitable adverse impacts of climate change and gender inequality. gender is already among the 11 forms of social impact which should be screened as part of any impact assessment carried out today by the commission before it makes any kind of proposal. as underlined in the report, gender-neutral policies start with gender-balanced representation in policy definition. decision-makers need to listen to women and make use of their knowledge in adaptation and mitigation strategies. however, while women constitute the majority of the world’s poor, women’s voices are often insufficiently heard. women are under-represented in national decision-making and in international climate negotiations. in the recent communication on a renewed eu-pacific development partnership it is stated clearly that gender inequalities may hamper countries’ capacity to address climate change impacts. this is particularly relevant in a region like the pacific, where women are under-represented in public decision-making and levels of gender-based violence remain high. pacific leaders acknowledge the need to take women’s experience, knowledge and priorities into account more effectively if they are to address the impact of climate change successfully and be able to achieve sustainable development. in line with the goal of raising women’s representation to the 40% level advocated in this report, women have chaired the last three conferences of the parties to the united nations framework convention on climate change (unfccc), and the durban conference of the parties adopted decisions explicitly referring to the need to ensure gender balance in the adaptation committee and the standing committee on climate finance. the eu needs to ensure gender balance among the members of these bodies as far as possible. the danish presidency decision to present the set of indicators on women and climate change to the council illustrates the fact that this critical area is high on the agenda. this is an important step towards developing a comprehensive toolkit for evaluating the gender dimension of decision-making and policies at all levels. we will need funding and capacity-building to enhance women’s knowledge of climate change and how to adapt their daily lives. it is our responsibility to give the concerns of women and the most vulnerable a place in national and international climate policy. the report before us is a very useful contribution in advocating climate equity: i commend it and i applaud the rapporteur and the european parliament for this initiative. i would like to conclude on this positive note: i truly believe that climate action can contribute to development in the world’s poorest regions and improve the living conditions of many men and women in the developing world. the commission will continue to mainstream gender issues into policy-making, including the fight against climate change and adaptation to the adverse effects of climate change.
the committee on the environment, public health and food safety has adopted a report on the proposal for a council directive on the approximation of the laws of the member states relating to the protection of the health of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. - first of all, thank you very much to our rapporteur. the poorest population in the world is 70% female. as the rapporteur and the commissioner have already said, climate change is particularly hard on women. they are the main victims of natural disasters and conflicts over, for example, natural resources. ironically, women are among the least climate-impacted population groups, and the share of women in decision-making in new climate strategies is actually far too low. but they are the most experienced experts and i think that they can make a good contribution to a better climate policy. there is still far too little knowledge about gender equality and climate change, but it is clear that more equality is better for the climate and a better climate contributes to more gender equality. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. that needs to change, and i would say: a smart girl is prepared for climate change!
- i'm the reporter. - ladies and gentlemen, we are in the midst of a financial and economic crisis and we cannot ignore it. the newspapers are full of it every day, the television news programmes are opened with it and the european council meets on virtually nothing else. but besides this pervasive crisis, we have another crisis, a very quiet crisis. a silent crisis of disappearing species, of disappearing habitats and of ever-growing oceans. that is the crisis we are discussing this morning. the figures are known; 25% of all species in europe are seriously threatened, only one sixth of all habitats in europe are in a favourable state, and as much as 75% of all fish stocks are overfished. according to the accountants of teeb, the economics of ecosystems and biodiversity, we lose 3% of our gross national product every year because of biodiversity loss. that would be 450 billion euros a year for europe! and that is very different from the one-off emergency fund for the euro that our government leaders have been working for two years now to raise that money. you could also say that we are talking about the largest multinational in europe today. because there is nothing that produces so much food, that provides so many services and provides us with so many products and so many jobs. today we are talking about europe's greatest service provider, nature! and any other multinational of that size would be politically sanctified, indeed, it would be too big to fail. but in the multinational nature of things, unfortunately, that often comes down to a few words. because let us be honest, we were also here ten years ago when we adopted the previous biodiversity strategy. and the loss of species would have stopped in 2010. we have failed miserably in this, and why? because in those moments when it really mattered, fine words proved to be of little value and other interests prevailed. so the big question today is: what are we doing differently than we have done in the last ten years to avoid having to find out in 2020 that we have failed again? let us begin with what we are legally obliged to do. is that too much to ask of member states? why should the citizen obey the law and not the member states? so it is not like the netherlands to refuse to restore nature in the western scheldt, as required by law, and then get angry when commissioner potočnik does not accept that. it is also of course very important that we integrate these environmental concerns into the other policy areas. we are having a unique moment! the agricultural policy is being reformed, the fisheries policy is being reformed, the cohesion policy is being reformed. half of europe's territory is agricultural land. without the active contribution of farmers, there is no nature. but i also say to my colleagues: without strong nature there is no agriculture, without bees there is no pollination, without fertile soil there is no crop. we must also develop and implement the no net loss principle. can anyone suggest a better way to halt the loss of biodiversity than simply stopping these activities that destroy nature? we also need to stop talking about incorporating natural capital in our national accounts, and simply start doing so: not words, but deeds! we must also dare to think bigger. many europeans think that true nature is only to be found in africa or the amazon. - that's not true! europe has a great natural heritage, but it does not yet have its own serengeti or yellowstone. but we can have it. there are areas of poor agricultural land, areas where people are moving away. let us invest in it in a different way and build it into new wilderness, into attractions for nature lovers. a real safari in europe, it can be! it also gives these desolate areas a whole new economic perspective. finally, mr president, i would like to thank the shadow rapporteurs for their constructive contribution to bringing the report to its present position. and of course commissioner potočnik, who does not fail to speak out for nature, which is important in europe and beyond. he did that in an impressive way in nagoya and will do so again at rio+20. the next step is to legislate. i am very much looking forward to helping him in this parliament and, in particular, to fighting with the council. colleagues, let us be careful with nature, the world's greatest and most important service provider. this multinational is all of ours! and one exception has already been made, state aid is possible!
a day of laughter is a day not lived! this report by mr gerbrandy is incredibly ridiculous. first of all, the sheer naivety of thinking that humans can manipulate the preservation of genetic diversity. secondly, it's very ridiculous to ask accountants to include ecosystems in their accounting system, say the flowers and the bees on the balance sheet. it is, however, irritating to hear that we should develop more wetlands, or swamps, in europe. that's asking for the return of scary diseases like malaria. it is ridiculous that mr gerbrandy is trying to create a european environmental police force, one that will come as a big brother to check whether we are actually complying with the costly, useless and harmful environmental rules of brussels. i also read that the eu must indoctrinate schoolchildren and students with the new biodiversity doctrine, that good agricultural land must be given up to ecosystems and that fisheries are being further hampered, while we do not even know what the state of fish stocks is. but the biggest joke is in his tail! mr gerbrandy wants the eu development cooperation commission to introduce the concept of biodiversity sustainability. i was unconscious! unfortunately, mr. gerbrandy believes his own rumours. i hope very much that this parliament will show more reason and not go along with his madness. and of course, mr potočnik, we are not going to flood good agricultural land in the netherlands, especially not in zeeland, because that would be a huge destruction of biodiversity.
there are still people in this house who think that biodiversity is just a "protect areas" thing. for those people who believe that, or still believe that, it's very important to know that biodiversity is the core of our food, the core of clean water. we all do! people who only shout that we should not protect areas, they still have not understood what the essence of biodiversity is, so mr hartong, do your homework a little better! various studies show that the biodiversity we destroy costs 3% of our gross national product every year. that is the value of biodiversity and that is what we have to talk about. we already see concrete examples: in agriculture, we see the bee population declining, and bees are crucial to our food supply! but also in fishing, yes, it's getting harder and harder to fish, but why is that? because we overfish! that is the problem and that is what we have to talk about. that is why we wholeheartedly support the commissioner with his biodiversity agenda for 2020, but of course there is a big problem if we only talk about targets for 2020. we need to talk about policy now! we are also talking in this house about our agricultural policy and our fisheries policy. let's get real about this. i therefore count on all those who are saying today how important biodiversity is in the real reform of our agricultural policy and our fisheries policy, so that biodiversity does not remain in words but is actually acted upon. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. the agreement was reached quickly, and that is what is needed, because the current rules are due to expire in a few weeks. the maximum tariffs in the agreement are, however, considerably lower than those originally proposed by the commission. the result is good for consumers, who will already notice the difference when they travel abroad this summer. calling is getting cheaper, texting is getting cheaper. but the real work, of course, is mostly in the manipulation of data. it's a major surgery. now it's what's crazy about it, say, about 3 euros per mb, and soon it's going to be much lower, namely 70 cents per mb. today is europe day and we celebrate europe's success stories of the past. indeed, intervention in the mobile phone market is one of europe's success stories, but it is a success story of the past. my question is: is that what we've done now the success story of the future? the commission has also proposed that the maximum tariffs should be gradually reduced to a single european tariff as from 1 july 2014. benefits: the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the proposal. the european parliament has also called for the eu to take action to combat the spread of the virus and to ensure that the virus is contained in the blood of all its citizens. as far as the pvv is concerned, this is too premature and must be preceded by a question. is there really an arab spring? i'll help you answer that question on the way. in tunisia, two bloggers were recently sentenced to seven years in prison for their atheist views. in the same country, tunisia, there is talk of criminalizing relations with the state of israel and of incorporating this into the constitution. in egypt, unfortunately, it's not much better. this year alone, an estimated 1,500 coptic girls have been abducted, circumcised and forced to convert to islam. the islamic sharia is also threatening to be introduced in egypt. i now ask you the question: do these developments really deserve the arab spring label? does anyone dare to use this term in front of all those women, christians and non-muslims who are oppressed? instead of an arab spring, an islamic winter is looming. i hope my colleagues will have the courage to face this. the emergence of fundamentalist islamic parties in the region is at odds with the values we embrace in europe. we must be careful not to make too light a decision to enter into trade relations with such regimes. we're sending the wrong signal. the message that the european union must send is that it will not deepen trade relations as long as regimes commit human rights violations or fail to act against them. we also owe it to the victims and the many vulnerable minorities in the region.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rules on the protection of the environment are respected. any breeder may freely use a variety for the development of a new variety (limited breeder's exemption) and may also freely market his new variety (general breeder's exemption). it is also necessary to include such a breeder exemption in patent law. it turns out that even varieties are indirectly patented, for example by a patent on a trait. in parentheses: i also reject such an exclusive claim to god's creation on principle. my first question to the commissioner - and i promise to wait until the end of the debate and therefore await your reply - is: is there any prospect of a revision of the biopatent directive? my last question concerns the unitary patent regulation. the itre joint consultative committee advised the juri committee, which was responsible for the matter, to include a limited grower exemption in the regulation. amendments to this effect, also by my esteemed colleague mr de lange, were unfortunately not adopted in the juri committee. what is the commission's view of a limited grower exemption in the regulation on cooperation in unitary patent protection?
i come from a country where, since the introduction of a handful of tulip bulbs, so to speak, some four hundred years ago, hundreds, if not thousands, of species and varieties have now been developed. the european agriculture and horticulture have grown up precisely by constantly reinventing themselves, constantly developing new and better species. this is not just true of my country, it is true of all the agricultural regions in europe. the innovative capacity of european agriculture, of agriculture worldwide, will only become more relevant. we're going to have nine billion mouths to feed, and so we need species that use less water and fewer pesticides and, for example, are more resistant to disease. our growers can meet that challenge, but we have to give them some space and clarity, and that is what is lacking at the moment. in the area of breeding law - and i am referring to the international upov convention, among other things, but also to article 4 of the bioprotection directive - we propose that people can use each other's breeds to breed, develop and discover new breeds. it's called the breeder's exemption. by contrast, patent law requires that any use of a variety be licensed by the patent holder. the rapid development of patent law in europe is jeopardizing the freedom of breeders, the freedom of development, and that must not happen. it is not ethically acceptable - and colleagues have already spoken about this - but it is also economically undesirable because it will paralyse any innovation. so i join my colleagues in calling on the commission to firmly enshrine the breeder's exemption in european patent law, or at least the limited breeder's exemption, which gives freedom to the whole process up to the point where you go to market and put a finished product on the market. it is therefore important to answer the specific questions put by mr belder and, if he cannot answer them himself, to have a colleague answer them in writing. we have already named a tulip in the netherlands after robert schuman, and that is a great honour for me on this european day. we also named a tulip after princess máxima. if the commission now sets about creating clarity in this area, who knows, commissioner, that will one day produce another 'tulip guchtiniana'. if i see you sitting like this, at least it'll be a nice pink one. i wish you the best.

rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on development and cooperation. - mr president, the role of parliament, as of any parliament, is to be the legislator, the voter and the policy monitor. we have those three functions. in the latter function, that of control of power, of control of the implementation of policy, we are much worse than in that of legislators or of voters. this is particularly true of our control of expenditure in the european development fund. it is a fund outside the eu budget, so much less transparent to us than the financing instrument for development cooperation. it is money that is spent outside the eu and that makes it even more invisible to us. we are still in a position to discharge it. that's very nice of us, but i don't think we should overestimate ourselves. the national parliaments do not carry out the control either. the european development fund should therefore be brought into the eu budget as soon as possible. that would strengthen our control capabilities. it's very unsatisfying to have to work like this. but if it is then brought into the eu budget, that should not be an excuse to reduce the whole of development money.
