in writing. - i do not support the fjellner report. the european court of auditors notes that the number of errors has increased over 2010 in the policy area which has had the highest error rate for many years, namely the structural funds, the so-called cohesion policy. the european parliament's report makes a number of justified requests to the european commission. i am glad, for example, that my amendment was adopted, which calls for the emergency fund to be checked for legality, regularity and effectiveness of expenditure. a missed opportunity, on the other hand, is that the granting of discharge is not made conditional on progress in the national declarations. they make member states more aware of their responsibility to handle the eu money they manage with care. this would help to reduce the error rate, as a substantial proportion of errors could have been prevented or corrected if member states had been sufficiently alert. it is a shame that ministers from countries in difficulty are turning down the invitation from the european parliament to talk about improvement. the good requests in the report can only be enforced by the european commission by postponing the granting of discharge. i regret that the committee on budgetary control has not taken this step.
- i'm the reporter. - mr president, criminal law is not just a subject. penalties are a restriction on the fundamental rights of the accused and the convicted. you have to be careful with that. of course, restrictions on fundamental rights are justified in certain circumstances, but they must be justified on the basis of good reasons, as laid down in international human rights instruments and the charter of fundamental rights. there are two threats, broadly speaking. first of all, we are the politicians. the media, more than ever, is devoting extensive attention to speaking crimes. lawyers are sometimes even more frequently on camera than politicians, and the prosecution and the judiciary today also employ an army of forgers. politicians may try to catch up by enacting new laws that either expand or redefine the crime, or increase the penalty. if they succeed in convincing their european colleagues that this legislation should be introduced throughout europe, they will have succeeded in their eyes. but it's not supposed to be like this. criminal law is not designed to be a short-term success story. before you agree to new criminal definitions or increase penalties, you should have thought carefully about the consequences. is it really bringing more criminals to justice and rehabilitation, and is public opinion still interested in these harsher punishments in the long term, or was this an incidental shock to society that could also be offset by better enforcement of existing law? the second threat, unfortunately, comes from brussels. we are used to making full use of the powers conferred on the eu by the series of treaties. the treaty lists a number of offences for which minimum harmonization is desirable. in addition, the eu may also harmonise minimum levels of crime as necessary for the implementation of already harmonised eu policies. this leaves a lot of room for manoeuvre, and i can think of a reason for any crime to be dealt with at european level. the same convention, however, also respects national traditions and cultural differences, which are reflected in criminal law. the emergency procedure, which is specifically provided for by criminal law and by which a member state may oppose harmonisation if it is contrary to the fundamental principles laid down in its constitution, is the procedural consequence of this. there is a tension between the two components, the broad powers and the restraint and respect for national traditions. that is why we cannot follow the same path in criminal law as in other areas: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. my report now makes it clear that in the field of criminal law, symbolism, that is, political hype, is not to be tolerated. before harmonization can be implemented, a test framework must be established which demonstrates the added value of harmonization through statistical data and, moreover, makes it clear that there are no other less intrusive solutions available outside the criminal law. and, in addition, human rights are still the decisive factor. this too will have to be explicitly checked at all times. equally important is the safeguarding of consistency and coherence. this will require special measures from all eu institutions, including the european parliament. the libe committee has no right to coordinate, but fragmented criminal law is bad criminal law. i thank all my colleagues on the libe committee for their overwhelming support for this report. we hope that we will have to develop these rules further in an interinstitutional working party with the commission and the council. i am confident that together we will succeed in ensuring that future criminal law rules are only introduced when they are absolutely necessary and that they continue to be of high quality.
rapporteur. − madam president, it is a great pleasure to be presenting to you today my third report on the situation of women in turkey, which presents a strategy towards 2020 and is inspired by the gender aspect of europe 2020. in preparing my report i spoke to various ministers, members of parliament, women’s ngos and many others, such as young university students. of all these meetings, the one that affected me the most was the meeting i had with arzu, a woman who was staying in the shelter. her story has helped me in evaluating the law on the protection of women from violence. i will come back to that. in my report i have made a call for zero tolerance for violence against women. the report indicates what needs to be done about violence and stresses that, without good implementation, even the best laws cannot protect women, so i put forward suggestions to ensure that the law is strictly applied. more shelters are needed. the existing shelters should be made safer, and the personnel must be better trained. there should be vocational courses offered to women in the shelters. this is important, because many of the women who have been victims of violence are housewives or, like arzu, have lost their jobs because their ex-husbands also attacked them at their places of work. arzu was continually beaten up by her husband even after obtaining a divorce. the law which was in effect at that time did not protect victims who were not married. it must be made clear to turkey that the law protects women, regardless of the relationship between the victim and the perpetrator. many positive developments are also taking place in turkey. the ankara prosecutor’s office has established a specialised bureau dealing solely with violence against women, which is very successful. we need more of these in turkish cities and rural areas. the gender gap in primary education has been closed, but there are worries concerning the new education reform. the new system should not jeopardise the successes achieved so far concerning the schooling of girls. the percentage of women in the labour market has risen to around 30%, but more needs to be done on issues such as childcare and maternity leave to further increase this ratio. trade union activities should also be made more accessible for women. in my report i ask for a new law on political parties and elections which would establish a temporary mandatory quota system ensuring the fair representation of women in electoral lists. in order to successfully make the necessary changes, a change in mentality is needed. women are not only mothers or members of a family, they are individuals. this must be made crystal clear. the commission is actively engaged in improving women’s rights in turkey. last week, during the launch of the positive agenda, commissioner füle once more highlighted the importance of women’s rights within the positive agenda, as well as the importance of chapter 23 negotiations on this subject. indeed, in order to give the eu more leverage in the area of women’s rights, chapter 23 negotiations should be opened. i want strongly to underline the importance of the commission and turkey continuing to promote of women’s rights in turkey and to place women’s rights at the heart of the accession negotiations. finally, i would like to thank everyone, especially the shadow rapporteurs, who have contributed to the drafting of this report.

in writing. - a tax on financial transactions is undesirable. that is why i tabled an amendment rejecting the proposal. unfortunately, the econ committee did not agree. in the amendment i referred to the analysis of the dutch court of auditors. it estimates that the cost to dutch banks, insurance companies and pension funds will be at least four billion, or 0.61% of gross domestic product. that is a large sum in view of the austerity measures which our member states are now undertaking. consumer spending and business investment are already under great pressure. it costs a lot of money and with it jobs and growth. the tax burden must not be increased unnecessarily. let alone the need for a european tax, part of the proceeds of which would go to the european union, and ultimately be paid by citizens and businesses. this is an unwise proposal and i therefore vote against it. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. a number of member states are against the tax. let those member states keep their feet to the grindstone.
in writing. - today i voted in favour of the proposal for a financial transaction tax. the sp is in favour of the introduction of such a tax. the sp, however, is against the eu receiving the proceeds. i would therefore like to stress that the revenue from this tax should be paid to the member states.
in writing. - i voted in favour of the final vote on the podimata report. i support the idea of a financial transaction tax as a way of making financial institutions pay for the economic crisis, for example through a tobin tax, the proceeds of which are used to support development and to combat climate change. the big problem with this report is that it proposes that the proceeds of the tax go to the eu, which is unacceptable. the taxation of goods is a competence of the member states and should remain so. the revenue from any tax on financial transactions should be used for the benefit of the member states themselves.

- i'm the reporter. - mr president, i should like to begin by mentioning a few figures which are probably dreamt of by those present here but not by many outside. that we have nine billion people on the planet in 2050, that we're seeing a doubling of the middle-income population to three billion in 15 years, that we need a planet and a half now for our consumption, and three planets in the future -- and we don't have that. that commodity prices have risen 150% in the last ten years, and that's with an economy addicted to cheap commodities. this is costing our economy billions, no, hundreds of billions more a year. so what we need in europe is an action plan, a concrete action plan to address this. the commission's road map, for which i would like to congratulate commissioner potočnik, is an excellent initiative. the commission's analysis and approach are very good. but what i still think is lacking is a higher degree of urgency and concreteness, and that is precisely what we are making clear as a parliament. that is not only the content of the report, it is also the structure of the report, in which we have identified six top priorities in the committee on the environment. i'd like to run through all six of them. first of all, mr president, it is very important that we come up with a joint action plan with industry to make europe much more efficient in its use of raw materials. the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the quality of the products it produces, and it is not surprising that the commission has taken a number of measures to improve the quality of the products it produces. the second top priority, mr president, is that we really need to move towards a single market for recycling and reuse. it should be easier to spread waste containing valuable raw materials across the border. the tax incentives for the use of secondary raw materials are also linked to this. secondary raw materials must ultimately be cheaper than primary raw materials, and this also requires fiscal incentives. the third priority, mr president, is to encourage research and development. we need to develop alternatives, we need to help the recycling industry to go even further in professionalizing. fourthly, we must extend the ecodesign directive. it's now focused on energy, but we also need to make sure that the raw materials that are in the products are more easily recoverable, and that starts with the design. fifthly, we need to integrate the concept, the idea of resource efficiency into the other policy areas; in agriculture, where we have to be much more efficient with water, in fisheries, and so on. and the sixth top priority is that europe should develop clear indicators and, as far as parliament is concerned, based on the footprint idea. measurement is knowing, and that's essential. mr president, as i have worked on this subject for some time, i have noticed that this is not primarily an environmental agenda. this is an economic agenda. we need to be more resource efficient if we are to sustain our economy in the future. i have already mentioned the hundreds of billions of euros that the rise in commodity prices costs industry every year. that is the real agenda, and that is why i am so surprised that the council - unfortunately not here - does not give a damn. that the council is not showing enough ambition to really get this action plan across europe, to move towards a much more efficient use of raw materials.
i have to say that i am very pleased that the commissioner has taken the initiative to put forward a proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos. at one point, one element was broken and i couldn't get the element repaired. it didn't even exist, and that kettle was actually still in good shape. but because we live in a throwaway society, buying a new kettle - not a very nice one, by the way - was cheaper. it's not supposed to be like this. we want to get rid of that. it is good that we are talking here once again about the finiteness of the earth and its resources, because it is still true, as the club of rome said in 1972 in its famous report 'limits to growth'. so now we're in 2012, 40 years later, and it's still the same, and it will be the same in 40 years. in short, it is time we did something about it, and got real. because in the last two years, we've presented at least two strategies for this: the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area. so ultimately, it's important that we start to integrate this into our society, and not in the way that people just have to put on extra sweaters in the winter or consume less, but in a way that appeals to everyone and ultimately leads to the efficiency that we're all talking about. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. it is often new models that prove to be leading edge and not new technology. so that's really what we need to focus on. better labelling, clearer and not five different labels: one for fair trade and the other for energy efficiency. can i please see at a glance what kind of product it is? improve the management of recyclable materials so that they do not end up in landfills or in incinerators. from abstract to concrete: europe's ecological footprint now covers ten football fields, and soon, with all the people there in 2050, there will only be one football field available for all the things we need - our energy, food and materials. so we have to be very, very fast. if i may give you one more example: in 2006, fertilizer cost $200, and now it costs $600. triple the price in six years. the urgency seems very clear to me. we have to do something. - right now. and not just for the environment, but for the economy, indeed.
on behalf of the green group, i would like to thank the rapporteur for the report. i looked up some financial statements from multinational corporations. akzonobel, a large multinational company, reports in its annual report: the greater rise in commodity prices has cost them a billion in profits. so a billion less profit due to higher commodity prices. in dsm's annual report, the company explicitly states that one of the major problems is the increase in the cost of raw materials, and that they have been able to pass on the higher prices to consumers so far, but that this is also becoming a major problem for dsm. unilever annual report higher commodity prices cost a profit of 2.5 billion last year. there, too, a multinational that indicates that the challenge of the future is: how do we deal with the dwindling of our natural resources? that's the schedule. that is not just a green agenda, that is also an economic agenda. does that mean that all the companies are really working on it right now? research shows that in the netherlands only one in five companies is working on a strategy on how to deal with these higher raw material prices. only one in five! in short, companies are still not sufficiently aware of the need to take a real look at how we are dealing with our natural resources. that's the whole point. in this sense we welcome the commission's programme, which sets out the right themes: how are we going to meet this challenge? by shifting the burden of labour to the raw materials, by looking at whether we are fixing tax amounts correctly, by implementing existing regulations, for example the waste directive. by integrating this policy into other policy areas, such as the agricultural policy we have in europe, and by working on good objectives and indicators. good texts, good agenda, but now is the time to take real action. that's what's gonna be important now. this green efficient economy could be the way out of the current economic crisis, but then we have to take it seriously. how are we going to look at the waste sector, for example? - i'm the reporter. - mr president, i should like to begin by thanking my colleagues for the very broad support for my report. that's very good for me. i've listened carefully and i've noticed a very broad consensus about the end goal: the more efficient use of resources. but we do not always agree on the route, and especially not on the pace at which we should do it. i want to say this about that: the speed we can afford is not determined by ourselves. we are not determining that in europe, but by the pace of the world around us. and let 's face it . the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for approval. there's been some criticism of eco-design. i have something to say about that. the text we are discussing today and voting on tomorrow is not a legislative text. this is a political text. a political text which sets out the main lines of our future policy. it is not therefore a detailed discussion of how to apply an ecodesign directive, but rather of the fact that we must also take into account the need to recover the raw materials used in the design of our products. that's the whole point. so not about a legislative text. i think the most important thing about this report, mr president, is that we as a parliament have sent a very strong political message, in which we also support the commission in continuing on the path it has embarked on. not just to go on, but to go on with an even more ambitious agenda than mr potočnik's. it is about implementation and it is about legislation. it seems in the discussion today that it's either/or; mr president, 55 years ago, equal pay for men and women was enshrined as the first fundamental right in our union. now, 55 years later, the pay gap is still unacceptably large. we're not doing something right, you'd think. a fundamental right is not enough in itself to change practice. for example, employers need to be much more transparent about salaries, so that women know when they're earning less than their male colleagues. salaries should be based on responsibilities and skills, not how hard you negotiate or how much you golf with the boss. labour inspectorates need to be much more rigorous in checking for pay disparities. and after 55 years, it is high time that we as the european union started enforcing that. i would very much like to hear from the commission when it intends to make a response.
welcome, commissioner, and thank you very much for your report, mrs bauer. according to recent data, women in the eu still earn 16.4% less than men for equal work, and legislation has been in place for 40 years to close the gap. however, in some member states the gap seems to be widening. in a so-called emancipated country like the netherlands, the average is even above the eu average. this wage gap is not just bad for the period that women work; we're also building up a lot less pension because of the wage gap. a staggering 22% of all women have an income near the poverty line after the age of 65. with the eu's non-social pension schemes now also aiming to cut state pensions, the number of older women in trouble will only increase. we have to act now. equal pay for men and women and pension schemes which worsen the position of women must be dropped. it is the only way to achieve a just standard of living for all: old and young, man and woman.

the euro crisis is unfolding like a wildfire, mr president, and the spotlight is once again on the fires that are still burning. that's what's scaring our citizens, and i understand that. however, we have made great progress. we have a fire brigade, which had to go out again last weekend for the spanish banks. but more than that, we need to get the euro house in order structurally. there has also been significant progress, although we are far from there. it is our task, and that of this european parliament as a legislative body, to contribute with determination and determination to strengthening the foundations of the euro. that is what our group is for. i would like to say to mr madlener and the pvv that there are no fairy tales. we cannot throw europe overboard, but we can preserve the prosperity and jobs of our citizens. there is no such fairy tale, and that is why we must continue to pursue it with determination. we have made important steps towards a fiscal union and an economic union; the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the commission can intervene when countries get out of hand. this package as a whole is important for the structural solution, for the structural restoration of confidence. i hope that tomorrow a large majority in this house will be prepared to take responsibility for this. president. - mrs thyssen is right. it is a one-to-one, with this package, but also with the crucial building blocks aimed at sustainable growth and jobs. i hope, commissioner rehn, that the council will also respect this common method. we will do our best to do so. president. - the appointment of a special representative for human rights offers opportunities, for example, to give greater attention to a human right which is crucial but which is under great pressure, namely freedom of religion. in many lands that freedom is in decline. in indonesia, the government does little to stop radical muslims targeting christians and ahmadis. in northern nigeria, the terrorist group boko haram bombs a church almost every week. unfortunately, there are countless other examples, but that is why attention to religious freedom is urgently needed. the eusr shall also provide obligations in addition to those opportunities. i am thinking of human rights, to take them seriously in relations between europe and other countries. this must be reflected in concrete terms in our trade agreements. in india, 40 million people are trapped in a life of slavery. a trade agreement between europe and india cannot therefore be concluded without a strong and effective human rights chapter.

mr president, clearly the topic of today is syria. everyday we hear of new horrible things, such as children being used as human shields. it shows the weaknesses that exist in the un system. another issue which demonstrates this is the long lasting israeli-palestinian conflict. we in the eu remain committed to a two-state solution, as confirmed in recent council conclusions on the middle east peace process. however, current trends are deeply worrying, especially concerning the use of administrative detention by the israeli government, which is used to imprison palestinians without charge or evidence against them. many prisoners are kept in isolated cells for 23 hours a day. despite declarations from the israeli government to end administrative detention, this practice is still going on. this is unacceptable in a country claiming to uphold the rule of law. mrs ashton, have you brought this issue up with your contacts in the region?

president, commissioners, there is a great deal in the country-specific recommendations with which i wholeheartedly agree. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to promote the development of the european social model, including the creation of a european social area. ngos, social partners, even national parliaments are hardly involved, so the newspapers are not full of it and voters do not care very much about what the commission has said when they go to vote. i challenge the commissioners to do a survey of national parliamentarians and then ask them how many of the five 2020 goals they can name. i'll bet you a big box of belgian chocolates that 90% of them couldn't name one. the democratic content of the semester must be raised so that member states can let the semester determine their policies much more, and i am not just talking about the objectives of sanctions, but also about all the other objectives. fortunately, it's an annual process, and we have a chance next year to just start doing better.
i would like to say that i am very pleased that the commission has taken the initiative to introduce a new system of taxation, which is to be introduced in the next few years. many have said it. when it comes to debt, there are two things that matter. first, not spending more than you earn, and second, looking for ways to earn more, to generate income. the internal market can help us in this. we must ensure that the internal market works, and that takes courage. less stifling rules, but better working rules in all member states alike and not in all different ways. experience shows that the transposition of directives creates a gap in the internal market, making it much more difficult to operate, especially for smes. i have been advocating for years that legislation for the internal market should be based on regulations, which work in the same way in all countries. so far i have not succeeded in doing so, but i ask you again to ensure that we base legislation for the better functioning of our european economy on regulations, which member states can no longer implement in a protectionist manner, which means that the crossborder economy does not work in the european union. the single market must once again become a model for the world economy, a place where money is made, where social security is provided, where a great many issues are discussed that ensure prosperity and political stability. we must ensure that we achieve a single market, mr president, where businessmen feel at home again instead of fleeing to outside europe because of the stifling rules, and where european citizens are proud to buy european products again. i see i have ten seconds left, so i'm gonna call acta. acta is dead because we haven't communicated enough to the citizens. but it is the european products that are being counterfeited for over a billion. we must make the internal market a place where entrepreneurs and european workers can feel at home again.
the european parliament has called for the european union to take action to combat poverty and social exclusion. i think that the creation of jobs must be our first priority. "towards a job-rich recovery" is a step in the right direction. i am very enthusiastic about the idea of a labour market monitoring system playing an important role in the jobs plans in the semester, and of course especially about the focus on 'green' jobs. we greens have read the working document on 'green' jobs with great pleasure. we could have written it ourselves. 100 000 to 200 000 additional jobs per percentage point of reduced raw material consumption, that should be the incentive for any government to adopt a green policy. but i don't have a lot of faith in that. our union is making a great leap forward: the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them. how can we force member states to pay attention to social matters when we have an excessive deficit procedure but not an excessive unemployment procedure, when more than 3% of the budget deficit can lead to sky-high fines but 50% of youth unemployment cannot. as countries, we are being charged for wage costs that are too high, but not for wages that are below the poverty line. we have to move forward and we have to move towards more integration. but if we are breaking taboos, why not break them socially? i would like to see the commission proposing a directive on the european minimum wage, on penalties for excessive unemployment or poverty, on making this initiative binding by 2020. courage, please!
president, commissioner, twelve years ago there was a major fireworks disaster in enschede in the netherlands; it made all the world's news. 23 people died in this. last year we had a major fire at chemie-pack in moerdijk, a company which had long been supplying incomplete information; it was just not maintained properly. the commission has also taken note of the fact that the council has not yet adopted a decision on the matter. it had been known for a long time what was going on at that company, but they had been given space for a very long time. every time a disaster like this happens, we're gonna amend the law. but above all we must keep a good eye on enforcement, because that is the most important thing. i am glad that this directive has now been revised, because it is very important for the citizen to be able to see what is happening in the neighbouring company and this gives him confidence. the fact that this information is now also available on the internet is very important in this day and age. it's a way for the public to access it. however, the problem remains that the provision of information is not always in one hand, that the municipalities or authorities do not always have the expertise to carry out the inspections properly. we really need to work on that. there is a need for more support for the authorities to carry out inspections properly and also to inform the emergency services. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the information provided to the public is correct and that the public is able to trust it.

author. − mr president, despite the challenges at home, the eu risks becoming too inwardly focused, even though events in the rest of the world matter for our security and prosperity. the southern neighbourhood is in the middle of a crucial transition and these events cannot be seen in a vacuum. a crisis is looming in the sahel region which risks having enormous spillover effects into the already fragile north african region all the way down to nigeria, which is facing instability every day. after the conflict in libya, weapons flowed through the sahel region way too freely, adding to the complex and intertwined problems of terrorism and extremism, poverty and organised crime, such as trading drugs, weapons and people. ordinary people, especially women and children, are the first to suffer, and they deserve our attention. we need to work together with the african union, the un and ecowas to speed up the implementation of the various components of the eu strategy for security and development in the sahel. we support the council’s commitment to contribute to the development of a peaceful, stable region where food security is guaranteed.

president. - the additional requirements for the digital tachograph in lorries have my support. that is good for road safety in europe. but if we really want to do something about road safety in the transport of goods, we need to come to a harmonized, article-based interpretation of the driving and resting time regulations. now many drivers are still stressed behind the wheel, because they can be fined for violations up to 28 days in the past. that's bad for road safety. so that is the point the commissioner should deal with first. the committee on transport of this parliament has rightly rejected the proposal to merge the driver's card with the driver's licence. that is an unnecessary invasion of privacy and when the card is taken, the driver is punished not only professionally but also privately. we cannot allow that to happen. that is not the intention. i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this. commissioner kallas, you have my support!
i would like to thank mr seeber for his excellent work. mr president, i do not know whether it is a coincidence or not, but today my own university, leiden, has come forward with the results of a major study of water quality in the netherlands which has been going on for many years. the conclusions are quite clear. first of all, until the year 2001, there have been very large improvements in the quality of water in the netherlands. the measure alone of taking up one and a half metres of space along the water's edges when using pesticides has improved the surface quality of the water by 70%. 70% with a fairly simple measure! but the other conclusion is that water quality has not improved since the year 2001, and that, according to the same study, is actually still relatively simple. with limited intervention, the water can be made 45% cleaner again. that says it all about the red line in mr seeber's report. that is implementation. implementation is the key to better water quality. and we are right to stress this. but also better integration. it has also been brought up again by commissioner potočnik. agriculture and industry use 90% of the water. there's still a lot of profit to be made. the more efficient use of water, it should be remembered, also fits into the roadmap for efficient use of resources. it's not unimportant. quite simply, we could use 40% less water than we do now. finally, the fourth element i want to bring up is the importance of ecosystem services. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the quality of water supplies is maintained, and that the quality of water supplies is improved. i hope and assume that mr potočnik will continue to do so and i look forward to the blueprint.
the commission's animal welfare strategy contains some good points, but a great many animals are left out of the current proposal. that is why i have added two points in an alternative resolution which parliament had previously supported by a large majority. the resolution is exactly the same as the paulsen report. the first addition is the eight-hour limit on animal transport. animals on their way to the slaughterhouse, that shouldn't take too long. over a million citizens have signed their names. efsa is in favour of shorter transport and a majority of the parliament says it is in favour of shorter transport. i hope the members will vote in favour of this on wednesday. the second addition is a ban on products from cloned animals and their offspring. the cloning technique is animal-worthy. more than 90% of all cloned animals are born dead or terminally ill. that's not the kind of food most citizens want. parliament has previously unanimously opposed this cloning technique. wednesday is the last chance to signal. please vote in favour of the alternative resolution.

mr president, we have been joined by millions of europeans who voiced protests against acta and i want to recognise this historic movement and build on it in a constructive way. now is the time for open, collaborative european democracy. acta is the symbol of closed politics, of non-transparent back-door decisions of the pre-lisbon era, and of hampering instead of embracing digital freedom. it is time to say goodbye to acta but – this rejection comes with responsibilities. we need to complete the eu’s digital single market, for which we desperately need copyright reform, not the enforcement of 27 different systems. we need sector-specific approaches to counterfeiting: a bag is not a movie. tangible goods are scarce; digital contact exists increasingly in abundance and we cannot enforce counterfeit goods the same way as we do digital content, not without violating fundamental rights or other collateral damage. internet service providers are not music police. let us foster more legal content instead.

in writing. − i abstained on the agriculture reports on alignment. these reports align the existing body of agricultural legislation to the new legal reality, which is the system of delegated and implementing acts. reports which use delegated acts are those on which the parliament has a right of veto. although i am opposed to the common agricultural policy, i recognise that the principle of protecting parliament’s right of veto, where it has been legally given (in the lisbon treaty), is a principle that needs to be protected. this principle will go beyond agricultural reports. because of this, because i do not wish the commission to make decisions in which parliament should co-legislate, i abstained on these reports.

the fact that there are problems as a result of schengen cannot be said enough here. the euro-philists in this parliament are blind and deaf and they do not want to understand. schengen is the cause of the mass displacement of crime and nuisance. now that the member states have finally recognised that these problems must be tackled, this parliament is opposing the solution. but border controls must be reinstated and member states must ensure security. those border controls were removed by naive idealism. this idealism does not bring about the hoped-for convergence within europe, but rather alienation. it's a cleft. the choices made by brussels do not offer solutions. brussels is voting for the failed euro experiment and thus against the citizen. brussels is in favour of increasing budgets and therefore against the taxpayer. and with schengen, brussels is choosing free movement of criminals over security. the european parliament supports these wrong choices. i therefore address mr van rompuy, through the representative of the council, as president of the european council. he does not like freedom, he does not like participation and he does not like national democracy, but he does like centralism, rule-making and japanese poetry. so i'm going to write a haiku for him. the schengen area. traveling crime. the line has been crossed. the winter in brussels.
i would like to respond to my pvv colleague who said, with a bit of condescension: "yes, schengen is a bit of an idea of idealists". my grandfather, zaliger, had a one-man import business for office supplies and travelled from the netherlands to brussels and paris every two weeks, initially on a bicycle, to collect his products. and i recently found his old passports that testify to what europe looked like before schengen. full of stamps, witnesses to long waiting times, the duty to declare foreign exchange, money to exchange. he would have embraced schengen because it would have made his life so much easier. speaking of idealism. the european commission, commissioner, must ensure that the schengen code is respected, not only in its letter but also in its spirit. and if there are eleven problematic situations in europe, it is clear that schengen is under pressure in the mind. it is at the heart of the european project. and if you say that the situation in the netherlands is not problematic, i am surprised. we are fighting crime not just in the border region, mrs malmström, but throughout the netherlands. so to join my colleague from greenleft: if it looks like border controls, it probably is border controls.
the principle of free movement of persons, goods, services and capital is one of the cornerstones of the european union. the treaty of lisbon has made the schengen agreement a core element of the european union. in an age of increasing nationalism and even populism, but also in an age of increasing crime and illegality, we must watch the schengen treaty very carefully and keep a very close eye on the interpretation of that treaty. i am personally in favour of a broad interpretation of schengen border controls - the commissioner knows that - but under community supervision. we have agreed that and that is a key element of european public law. in this respect, i am also shocked by the decision of the european council of 7 and 8 june to abandon or to limit community supervision, or community method. and i welcome the commitment of the president of the european council to restore that. but we will judge him by his deeds. parliament, and i agree with all the people who have spoken before me, cannot be left out of the picture on this point. (the speaker is prepared to answer a 'blue card' question (rule 149 (8) of the rules of procedure)).
"blue card" question. what is it? i have here a letter from the dutch minister for foreign affairs, a member of mr. van de camp's party, dated july 3, 2012, which says that as of august 1st, he will intensify the mobile foreign surveillance or the mobile security surveillance - they change their name quite often. is that the way mr van de camp would like to interpret the schengen agreement broadly?
"blue card" is the answer. the commissioner said this afternoon, if i have understood him correctly, that a first assessment of the mobile surveillance of foreign nationals by the european commission is not contrary to the schengen treaty. if this mobile surveillance of foreigners is to be misused to impede the free movement of european citizens, we are on the wrong track. but if the dutch minister says, precisely because of the problem of illegality, that i am responsible for mobile surveillance of foreigners and i also know that there are dutch judges who have difficulty with this, then i think that the dutch minister can continue, but again, under community supervision.

awareness that business as usual will not deliver the growth that countries need, and that greening the economy is a necessity. even the countries that were deliberately trying to get rid of all blue-card question. – mr szymański, it may be the translation, but did i really hear you say that people who are suspected of – maybe – committing terrorist acts cannot have the same rights as other citizens? does that mean that you reject the principle that human rights are universal and indivisible? if so, does that also mean that you do not subscribe to all the values that we have enshrined in our treaties?

(the president agreed to answer a 'blue card' question in accordance with rule 149 (8) of the rules of procedure)
blue-card answer. – mr president, i will react to that question and not the other one. i am hearing different things here. you are mixing up what is happening in the baltic sea area and the mediterranean. i do not think there is much competition when you look at transport from the northern and southern parts of europe. it is also not true that there will now be a lot of transportation by trucks because of this legislation, because trucks already have far higher standards for lowering sulphur emissions. i therefore do not see the point you are raising. i also think it is high time for the shipping industry to emit less sulphur, just as trucks are doing right now.
there is certainly no perfect, but a reasonable compromise on the table. it is of course a pity that low-sulphur marine fuel is not mandatory on all european seas. now only the baltic sea, the channel and the north sea are benefiting from the low sulphur content, and that is a pity. but at the same time, it has prevented all the other seas in europe from having to meet the requirements at all. a number of agreements have also been concluded on these seas. this is the maximum that can be achieved at the moment, and i also think it is right that we in parliament should make a clear statement on the so-called shore-side electricity supply. i think that is a huge gain for the inhabitants of a number of ports in europe, such as rotterdam. so, in short: this is the best we've got. i support the hassi report.
many people have said today that it is incredibly good that we are finally taking steps to save energy. it is insane that europe is lagging behind in terms of energy saving, in terms of using less energy, the cheapest, easiest and best option of the climate package that europe has adopted. it is very good that we are taking action there now. but then we must continue with this. i have a question for mr oettinger: when will the ecodesign directive be implemented for boilers? we know that the proposal is there, but when will it come now? that is a very important step to be taken and i expect a concrete answer to my question. the second point is the emissions trading system. there's an incredible amount of air in the emissions trading system, there's a lot of surplus in it, and it's only going to get more. when will the commission take concrete action to remove air from the emissions system so that we can continue to commit ourselves to that part of europe's climate policy?
the power of brussels is fortunately limited by subsidiarity and the treaty. subsidiarity means that brussels does not interfere in matters which the member states can manage themselves. the swedish parliament has officially protested against this directive, but brussels, as always, ignores the interests and sovereignty of the member states. article 194 of the treaty on the functioning of the european union explicitly limits what brussels can do in the field of energy. the article says that brussels cannot interfere with the operation, choice or structure of the energy supply in a member state, whether it is efficient or not. the current proposal on the efficient use of resources is therefore not only contrary to the principle of subsidiarity but has no legal basis whatsoever. why are we voting on this? what brussels is talking about is the organization of the european union itself. and there are certainly efficiency gains to be made. we have huge numbers of civil servants, we get one new agency after another. we have an unimaginably expensive but utterly failed euro policy and all of that is unnecessary. we can at least use our energy more efficiently by talking only about what we are talking about. and let us put an end to this parliament.

for the epp group late last week, jacques beres, co-founder of médecins sans frontières, returned to paris after two weeks of intensive medical care in a hospital in the front city of aleppo. in a candid interview, he said he had treated mostly rebel fighters, at least half of whom were of foreign origin. beres' patients included dozens of fanatical jihadists - this has already been brought up in the debate - including young french citizens. they are seeking the establishment of an islamic state in syria, based on the shari'a. i would like to put two specific questions to you, madam high representative: does the commission share jacques beres' findings on the large number of jihadists on syrian territory and, if so, what implications this has for the european strategy in syria? secondly, how do you intend to deal with the return of european jihadists from the syrian battlefield in the near future?
mr president, after so many debates on syria, i already know what we are going to be hearing from you: it is a terrible tragedy; human rights have to be respected; assad has lost his legitimacy; the eu has imposed sanctions and provided humanitarian aid in cooperation with our partners, etc. etc. but this means nothing any more for the syrian people who are being slaughtered every day, caught between a murderous regime and foreign fighters ranging from hizbollah to al-qaida. i do not think assad is losing sleep over eu sanctions. the options are not easy, but doing nothing is no longer one of them. let us at least enforce our own embargoes effectively. you will understand the shock i experienced at reading the council conclusions on 23 july, one year and two months after eu sanctions and an arms embargo were imposed. i quote: ‘eu countries will be obliged to inspect vessels and aircraft heading to syria if they suspect they contain weapons or equipment for internal repression’. is this what we are coming up with as a result of a council, one year and two months after the imposition of an eu arms embargo? i do not think this does any justice to the eu as a global player. words are no longer enough for the syrian people. blue-card answer. – indeed the role of cyprus is very important here. there have been ships which have docked in cyprus that have later continued on with weapons on board to syria. that is a disgrace. i am surprised, and confused about what a weapons embargo even means if this can continue to happen. if we are not capable of at least enforcing our own actions and words, such as on a weapons embargo and the export of repressive technologies, then we are losing our credibility. i appreciate the complexity of the international reality around syria but i do think that doing nothing – the way we have for the past year and a half – is no longer an option.
i am very pleased that the commission has taken this initiative. we have also written that in the resolution we have negotiated today. we are both incredibly critical of russia's democratic development. we also know that that country has never had a real democracy and that it is also a learning process that they have to go through. but where we thought we could hope and trust that president medvedev would take office in the right direction, things are now going in the opposite direction. we agree with you that modernising russia means making it more prosperous, but also, and above all, that this leads to the democratization of russian society. it is not just us europeans who see that things are not going well, that the situation is stagnating, it is also the russians themselves. we were in russia a week and a half ago with the eu delegation and we spoke in st petersburg about civil society. we also discussed all the laws you just mentioned. the problem is that you never know where you stand because of the vague and very broad interpretation of civil society . this makes the proper functioning of civil society absolutely impossible. the organizations do not know at what point they can be sued. what are we going to do together to ensure that the rule of law is also respected? that is the only way to modernise.
the fisheries policy must be reformed, because it is all too much and too tightly regulated from brussels. one of the things that is important is sustainable innovation, also in fisheries. unfortunately, sustainable innovation in fisheries cannot always take place. i am referring specifically to the fishing technique of pulse fishing. in this way of fishing , the fish are startled from the sea floor with electric shocks of a few volts . the advantage of this is that the seabed is not damaged and the fuel consumption of the ships is reduced by 40%. there's a lot less by-catch and a lot more yield. so perfect! but unfortunately, at the moment, all forms of electric fishing are prohibited and so is this pulse fishing. there is a temporary exception, but i am calling on the commissioner, too, to make room in the new fisheries policy for this new, innovative and sustainable fishing technique.
there is no doubt that policy must be practicable. the overarching report by mr salafrakos rightly points out that the prohibition of discarding with the landing obligation for mixed fisheries is very problematic. compromise proposals for the basic regulation will be worked on in the near future. let us therefore make sure that there are no unworkable rules in the regulation. it would be wrong to put the mixed fisheries sector under pressure by imposing an unworkable discard ban. so let us see what objectives for mixed fisheries are feasible and at the same time ambitious.
for the greens/efa group first of all, i would like to thank mrs jiménez-becerril barrio and mrs parvanova, who have joined forces here to put forward a new european directive which is really going to change the european criminal procedure. i've had a long time to think about it myself and get used to the idea that our justice system is going to change by empowering victims. but i've gotten used to the idea and i've come to find it charming. it is also crucial for the greens that in this new law for victims we do not distinguish between people who have legal status and people who do not. for example, if you are a victim of human trafficking and you are in the european union without documents, this directive should ensure that you report it and that you are assisted in your criminal proceedings. so, we think it's crucial that you get shelter, translation, medical care and psychological support. but this leads - and this strengthened european cooperation in the field of justice is now a clear example - to a shortage of legal aid. this really calls for european rules on legal aid, because obtaining your rights on paper is something different from actually obtaining them in court. victims will feel that way, suspects already do. but without minimum standards for legal aid in the european union, this is not enough.
for months, we have been watching from the outside world a political soap opera in romania: one politician after another is accused of corruption, fraud and other shady deeds. romania is currently showing itself politically as a kind of banana politics. but we already knew that. the romanian politicians will certainly not receive awards for decisive action and transparency of governance. but these are romanian problems, and they are the ones that the voters in romania are voting for year after year. it may be a mess, but it's their mess, their own sovereign choice. the european union must keep out of internal political turmoil and elections. the european commission is constantly crossing its borders by imposing eu dictates on sovereign states. as soon as there are elections in sovereign countries, the eurocrats rush over one another to interfere in those elections. eu commissioners from the countries concerned are welcome to be used. in the netherlands, kroes had to put her euro-philosophy in the papers if necessary, and in finland olli rehn was never far away. in romania, mrs reding and mr barroso felt it necessary to get involved in internal affairs. this is unacceptable behavior for unelected officials, who should not interfere with democratic processes in sovereign countries.
i am listening to this debate with increasing amazement. we have had a few debates in this house, first on the situation in italy, then on the situation in hungary and now in romania. it's so transparent how the cards are being shuffled. this time mr weber of the ppe group praises mrs reding for the investigations she is conducting on romania and mr swoboda says that the popularity of the president of romania has been greatly diminished. we are taking sides, and this is because we are still not all of us in agreement on the basic rule that the copenhagen criteria should also apply to those countries which are already members of the european union. we keep on catching each other's flies here in this parliament, we suggest that it is partisan debates because we refuse to judge people by what we should judge them by. my question to you is: if the elections are held on 9 december, will we have mrs reding's research in front of us? or should we wait for the elections there and watch these undemocratic tendencies unfold without further details?
there is still a persistent lack of confidence in our banking sector, and small and mediumsized businesses are paying the price, because they have difficulty borrowing money. and that is crucial for growth and jobs. i commend commissioner barnier for the proposals he has presented today. the direction is right, the ecb has the power to intervene. but we have to look at how this is set up. a few points of interest. we must not disturb the single market, but strengthen it. this means a strong role for the eba as the guardian of the single european rulebook. but how will that work in the future? is it revoked by the ecb with additional capital or liquidity requirements? how does that compare? and also, should we extend banking supervision as quickly as possible to as many banks as possible in as many member states as possible? that is my second concern. because despite what you say, there is a danger of a wall being erected between banks in countries of the euro zone and outside. there are also banks that have been infected by the crisis virus and there are also many countries, such as poland, hungary and others, that would like to join. we must take this seriously, because we want stability throughout the european union. some member states are already making efforts to dismantle the proposals. but we must strengthen those proposals, and you need the european parliament to do that! that was the case when it came to the establishment of the european supervisory authorities. this was the case in the decision-making process on the six-pack for economic governance. we will play our part in the legislative process, including on proposals where we have a right of opinion, as we have shown in the six-pack. with this package we want to move forward with you to make it as strong as possible for a stronger europe.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on the internal market and consumer protection. - i thank my colleagues and the rapporteur. i was the rapporteur for an opinion in the committee on the internal market. it is still a pity that this is a directive rather than a regulation, because as you know i am a strong supporter of regulations, which work in the same way in all member states. now, however, you get the different implementations, and that's a shame.
it will be difficult to defend for the general public, when all over europe there are cuts to be made in order to increase the budget of the european union by 6.8%. the alde group, however, believes that it is a consequence of commitments made in the past. contracts are supposed to be honored. if the payments were really to be reduced, there is one very simple solution: i think that the commission should be more careful in its approach to the problem. the alde group has tried to do this in this budgetary procedure. we have said that for those countries which are misusing their structural funds the obligations could be reduced by 5%. unfortunately, this proposal could not be supported by a majority and we have not tabled it again. but we are very much of the opinion that commitments must be honoured. if the budgetary authority, the council and parliament, have first voted to implement certain projects and the contracts have been concluded, then it is not possible to say afterwards 'unfortunately we have to cut back now' and 'we do not have the money'. it can't be! a government that respects itself must honor its contracts. in addition, every year money is being returned to the member states, and that is because the accounts which we are now presented with were not presented early enough. the alde group does not believe that if these bills are submitted late, they should not be paid. we therefore find it very difficult to defend this 6.8% increase in the budget. we are fully prepared to listen to the council, we are always prepared to be persuaded, but at the moment we think that the commission's arguments are stronger than the council's arguments. i would also like to ask the council: is it true that the original estimates submitted by the member states were even higher than the 6.8% that the council is now asking for? i would like to have an official reply from the council. were the original estimates of the member states, which the member states requested individually and not collectively, higher than the 6,8% or not? as things stand, we will support the position of the committee on budgets.
mr lewandowski, since i arrived in strasbourg yesterday, i saw a number of elderly gentlemen playing football and children playing skateboard and football in the sun at noon. i have to think of them today as we discuss the 2013 budget. how do i explain to them that the commission wants to increase the 2013 budget by 6.8%? i can tell them that the council of ministers was subsequently angry about that increase and did not want to agree to it. but my explanation becomes impossible if i have to tell them that this european parliament wants to increase the commission's increase even further, and even to almost 7%! i cannot explain that, mr president, and i do not want to. we cannot burden our parents who built our countries and our children who hope to live in them in the future with such a terrible budget proposal from the commission and this parliament. the most sensible thing we can do today is to throw the commission's proposal line by line into the trash and ask for a new budget proposal, this time with substantial cuts and a sharp reduction in the 2013 budget. the pvv cannot settle for less and i hope that neither can any sensible member of the european parliament. the proposed 2013 budget is a disgrace and i reject it outright on behalf of the dutch citizens who, according to mr schulz, are staring at the navel.
governments and citizens across europe and in large parts of the world are being forced to cut back. but what do we see? the committee on budgets here wants to increase the budget by almost 7%. you're wondering on what planet this unimaginable decision was made. my group has tabled amendments to reduce the budget. we must abandon the megalomaniacal project of the house of european history. the european agencies and the european external action service must also be checked to see if they are spending the budget wisely. why, mr president, has the european railway agency had two workplaces 45 kilometres apart? why does the european external action service have almost 50 staff on the sunny island of barbados? at least that's how important transparency is. and that has to start with ourselves. i have again proposed that the general expenses allowance for members should be made transparent. if we include this point in the resolution, the european parliament will gain credibility, and i think that is very important.
since the arab uprisings, there has been a worrying increase in anti-semitic expressions in the mediterranean countries and this poison must be fought. i have therefore tabled two amendments aimed at combating anti-semitism in the context of financial cooperation with the mediterranean countries. i am very, very pleased that the committee on budgets has adopted these amendments. but this result, mr president, must not be changed. the epp group has in fact requested that we vote on the anti-semitism clause. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the amendments are nos 1241 and 1192 to the budget lines 1908-01 and 1908-02.

the rapporteur. - mr president, in addition to the excellent report by mr charles goerens, i should like to make three comments. the agenda for change rightly calls for a better link between emergency aid and development policy. in my own country, the netherlands, there are votes to limit our road to emergency aid, which is an absurd idea. now, providing only emergency aid in, say, mali is disastrous and not in our best interest. looking no further than the hunger among the refugees in northern mali means pure profit for the muslim fanatics who are imposing sharia law despite strong protests from the local population. providing al qaeda with a safe haven, making northern mali a hotbed, an afghanistan close to our borders. turning a blind eye to northern mali also benefits latin american criminals who are increasingly free in west africa to transport cocaine across the sahara to europe via the ports of ivory coast and guinea-bissau. to do so means to support the peace operation and to work actively in mali to build a future for the region, including the tuaregs. president, in 2015 we must have global agreements on how we will ensure that after that year everyone has the chance to get out of poverty. that requires a public debate. it would be good if the european union declared 2015 the year of development cooperation. i would like to hear from commissioner piebalgs if he can help us to get this done. finally, without open democracy, equal opportunities are an illusion. as long as the poorest have no voice in their capitals, their interests are not respected there. that is why it is so important to invest in making the voice of the poorest heard, in local and regional parliaments, in civil society organisations such as ngos of minorities and of women. the european commission is taking this approach, supported by the european parliament. i hope the council will also agree with this.
i am very pleased that the commission has taken this initiative. but what always seems to me to be a problem is that in times of economic crisis we change our view of our development cooperation policy. that could very well be the case if we were very, very clever and if we said that our starting point is policy coherence and that we are going to put real pressure on trade and investment in development cooperation. but if we do not do that and if we are really looking at how we can use our official development aid funds, then i am still very much afraid, mr piebalgs, that we are in the process of organizing the arguments for the opposition, and that is: you can do it more efficiently, so you can do it with less money. i see that in my own country, where the contribution to development cooperation has fallen from 0.8% of gross national product to 0.7% of gross national product, and i will see that in more countries. my question to you, mr piebalgs, is therefore also: when are we gonna do something smart? when will we start to take honest trading and honest investing as our starting point?
the rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on budgetary control. - the committee on budgetary control naturally considers that the most important thing is that the money is spent properly. this requires the court of auditors to issue a positive das, a positive declaration of reliability. we hope that the das will not be negative again for the umpteenth time in november. the committee on budgetary control strongly believes that the national member state declaration is the means to achieve this. someone in a member state must take political responsibility for the eu money that is spent. the new financial regulation which we are likely to adopt today creates the possibility for this. we in the committee on budgetary control believe that the commission should take this opportunity to encourage member states to make such a declaration and to treat those member states which do so better in terms of controls than those which do not. so , what do you say ? the stick and the carrot as they say in english.
president, mr lewandowski, yesterday we were talking about the 2013 budget, today we are talking about the budget for the years after. fortunately, the british prime minister has already threatened to veto the multibillion-dollar budget, and i advise our dutch prime minister rutte to do the same. - why? - why? to teach the commission that threats to national parliaments cannot go unpunished. to remind the commission that it is those same national parliaments that decide on the purse of europe and not the euro-clique here. to call for an end to all these committees and agencies which do nothing to contribute to national objectives. and above all, to let the commission know that it must not think that european taxes will ever be passed. if the commission does not want to listen, it must feel. fortunately, the netherlands, as a member state, has the right to veto, as does the united kingdom. as far as the pvv is concerned, i'd like to see it as soon as possible.
