the council's conclusions are another exercise in opening doors. the simulation of growth, the implementation of the 2020 strategy and the further elaboration of the eu, we have all heard it before. you can hold as many meetings as you like, but as long as you stick your head in the sand over the fundamental problems of the european union, the situation will only get worse and worse. a single currency for a continent of such diversity is doomed to failure. we must cherish this diversity and not turn our backs on even more centralisation and control from brussels. that's not worth the euro. moreover, a majority of citizens in europe do not want to transfer more powers to brussels. everybody knows that, unless you're blind. we are all democrats, aren't we? why are these social signals not taken seriously? what must happen before you finally come to your senses and reach the inevitable conclusion that you can't go on. more economic contraction, unemployment and social unrest? this project is completely derailed politically, economically and democratically. the european parliament has a duty to ensure that the european union is not left to its own devices. that is a good thing for democracy and a chance for economic recovery.
the summit discussed banking supervision and the plans call for the ecb to become the supervisor. but by buying up large-scale government bonds of dubious quality, the ecb has become part of the crisis. the ecb must first regain its independence by no longer inflating its balance sheet with government and bank debt. the ecb may then exercise banking supervision, provided that banking supervision and monetary supervision are functionally separated and provided that such separation does not imply that banking supervision should be placed under the political control of the european parliament. the stability and growth pact has shown that political control does not work. moreover, banking supervision is not the cure for all problems, because the continuing concern about greece, portugal, spain and italy forces us to have a lively discussion about the future of the euro. in addition, painful solutions must also be seriously discussed.
president of the commission. − mr president, first of all regarding the assessment of this european council, some of you have probably noted that in my initial statement i mentioned three european council summits. i did that on purpose because this summit was important in terms of confirming some earlier decisions. that represents some progress already because the reality is that, unfortunately, doubts existed as to the commitment of some governments regarding decisions they had taken previously. in fact, though, the final decisions on some of those most important matters will come later, namely regarding the emu in december and regarding the mff, hopefully, in november. so my assessment of this european council is that it was certainly a step forward in terms of previous decisions being confirmed, but it was not yet the comprehensive response needed for the current challenges in the euro area and in the european union. i consider this a fair assessment of the results of this european council. of course we have to know that we are working on extremely sensitive matters where, to be frank, the initial positions of our member states are very diverse and where the interest, i believe, of all the european institutions is to create the right conditions for consensus and operational decisions. one point where there was some progress was on the single supervisory mechanism (ssm) and i am also glad that this debate today has shown a broad degree of consensus on the principles set out in the commission proposals and reflected in the european council conclusions. these include, most importantly, that the ssm should cover all banks, that there needs to be openness – as full as possible – and to have as full as possible participation by the non-euro member states which so wish, and that the ssm is a key element in proving that the european union is capable of moving forward, of restoring confidence, deepening the emu and opening up the way to decisions on bank recapitalisation by the esm. i particularly welcome the determination expressed by parliament, namely by rapporteur ms thyssen, to advance the ssm work so that we can reach a quality result by the end of 2012. the commission will work closely with you to further improve the ssm proposals and open the way for the operational phasing-in of the system in 2013 – as had in fact been proposed by the commission – starting with systemic banks and eventually extending to all banks in the euro area and participating non-euro area member states. another important issue that was mentioned here – i think it was a question by mr mauro – was the sense of urgency. i want to tell you that i made that urgency clear. the reality once again, if you want to be honest in our assessment, is that not all member states feel the same degree of urgency. this is probably due to the fact that they are in different financial and fiscal positions, but i believe that it is important for the credibility of our response that this response is properly prepared. some of these issues are extremely difficult from a political and technical point of view but the decisions should be as urgent as possible. another issue that was raised concerned a social pact for europe. how can we complement some of these decisions with proper consideration of the social dimension? the elements proposed in the social pact are, to a large extent, already reflected in the europe 2020 strategy. that is, let us not forget, a strategy for inclusive growth, and in fact the macroeconomic imbalance procedure has been an integral part of the implementation of this strategy during the second european semester. this has implied a number of new challenges for all of us, notably in terms of ensuring that the labour market and social issues are adequately reflected and assessed within this new framework. this integration results from the fact that economic growth and employment and social aspects are two sides of the same coin. it was interesting to see in the debate that many people are speaking about having a real minister for finance in europe. they should probably also be thinking about having a real minister for the economy in europe, so that we complete the work on financial stability with a commitment to sustainable growth and also to the economic and social dimension. employment and social cohesion are not just an outcome of economic growth but are also an important determinant. the commission strongly believes that our values of social justice, solidarity and equal opportunities must remain at the heart of european integration and continue to be understood as prerequisites for our future economic prosperity, for the well-being of european citizens and for european cohesion. they must also appear in the european union’s growth agenda. at the beginning of 2013 the commission will present a social investment for growth and cohesion package which will also address various aspects mentioned under what some of you have described as the social pact. the message i want to convey to you is that, yes, we are doing everything we can with the tools and instruments available at european level to inject this economic and social dimension into the broader response to the current crisis in the economic situation of the european union. regarding the future responses on mutualisation of debt, you know what our position is. the commission has stated it and we will also address this issue in the blueprint i have mentioned. one point i want to make clear, and that i do not agree with, is the position of those who say that they are in favour of the content of the two-pack, they are in favour of more integration in supervision of budgetary policy, they are in favour of reinforcing the competence of the commission and the european institutions, the community method, in terms of economic governance, but they will not support this – they will not approve it – before decisions are taken on other issues like the redemption fund. this is a mistake. it is a mistake because if you make progress on one very important issue in the european union dependent on other issues, where you know very well that there is not yet agreement between all our member states, you are in fact paralysing progress and economic governance in the euro area. that is a choice for you to make, but my strong advice to all the pro-european forces is to support everything that enables us to put as much as possible under the community method and in the european institutions and this is certainly the case with the two-pack proposals. now another issue which was mentioned was the situation regarding some statements made by the imf. i want to be clear on this aspect as well. the reason we have this crisis today, contrary to what some euro-sceptics suggest, is not because of the euro area. we have a crisis today in the european union economy because of excessive debt created by national governments and because of the irresponsible financial behaviour in some sectors, including outside of europe. so europe was not the cause of the problem, europe is indeed the victim of the problem and is now trying to be part of the solution. this is the reality. (applause)it was irresponsible behaviour in terms of the excessive debt of public authorities and the sometimes completely irresponsible behaviour of some actors in the financial sector which created the problem. the proof of this – that this is not the problem of the euro – is that, for instance mr farage, your own country, which is not a member of the euro, is now implementing a very difficult austerity package. the british people are suffering the consequences of past mistakes. it was not the euro that created the britain’s problems. (loud applause)so it is intellectually – i repeat – it is politically and intellectually dishonest to pretend that the euro is the cause of our problems. in fact, it is the european union that is part of the solution because, without the support of other countries in the euro area, some of our member states would not have the financing necessary to keep their states running, to keep their social services, to keep their national health systems, to keep their public education. this is the reality. so europe is part of the solution and now we have to do everything we can to stabilise the euro area and to create the conditions for growth. but what growth? sustainable growth! not the growth of the past. the growth fuelled by excessive debt is not sustainable. the growth fuelled by irresponsible private credit is not sustainable, as we have seen. we have to return to conditions for growth that is sustainable. this why the imf, like the commission, like the ecb, is in favour of fiscal consolidation. what the imf said, and we can completely agree with this, is that the situation is different in some member states and so those countries which have the margin to promote growth in terms of internal demand can do so, and in some cases there can be fine tuning of the objectives. indeed i want to make it very clear that it was the commission which proposed, and we were supported by the imf, but it was the commission which proposed one more year for portugal and spain to reach the budget consolidation targets. it was our proposal, not the imf’s proposal. so, first for spain and then for portugal, the commission recommended to the council that the deadline for correction of excessive deficit be extended by one year. our fiscal rules, the revised stability and growth pact, allow for this: when the economic situation turns out to be worse than expected and the reduction of the deficit in structural terms is taking place, an extension of the period to correct a nominal deficit can be granted. this is not automatic. the situation will have to be assessed on a case-by-case basis. this was also the position of madam lagarde when she said ‘conseil nécessaire et possible’ which means in english: advice ‘when it is needed and possible’, and this is exactly the position taken by the institutions that are monitoring the situation in some of our countries. in particular, in the case of countries under financial assistance, namely greece, ireland and portugal, one must assess the consequences on that sustainability of a possible prolongation of an adjustment period. prolonging the adjustment period can delay reversing the increase in the government’s debt ratio and put it on a declining trend. this in turn will delay the return of those countries to the market, possibly requiring extra financing, and then comes the question: are the other euro area countries – because decisions are taken by the governments – the commission makes the proposal in the end but i want to make it clear that the decisions are taken ultimately by governments – are the euro area countries ready to provide extra financing? this is an important issue. this means that for programme countries the possible prolongation of the programmes is subject to the severe constraints stemming from high levels of debt. these are also in fact the views of the imf. now, one thing it is important to note and which people have not paid sufficient attention to, is that some of those who are now saying that we should be more, let us say, flexible in terms of the fiscal targets, are exactly the same people as are proposing hard restructuring of debt; are exactly the same people as believe that the euro area is too big, that southern europe should not in fact be in the euro area, that it was a mistake to have those countries in the euro area. this is exactly the point i want to make: we do not agree. we believe we should do everything we can to keep the integrity of the euro area because when you accept more flexibility in terms of fiscal adjustment you have to think what the consequences in terms of deficit stability will be and how we can keep those countries going to the market as soon as possible so that they can finance their own state. it is very important to understand this. this is why in fact i wanted to respond to those direct questions from you, to say that a lot remains to be done, but i believe that this european council was an important moment for discussing the difficulties in a relatively open manner. i am not underestimating in any way the difficulties and challenges ahead. i think we are now coming to a moment where, probably because there is less pressure coming from the markets, the sense of urgency in all of our capitals is not the same. that is why i expect parliament and the commission, as european institutions that keep firmly to the agenda, to understand that, without a complete comprehensive response to the euro area governance, we will not have the necessary conditions of confidence and confidence is critically important to restore investment and growth and jobs. this is our final and ultimate goal. growth and jobs, but i repeat, sustainable growth for sustainable jobs.
the european council - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, many of you have spoken and said: 'we must act, we must act now'. i have given you an overview, as you well know, of everything we have done in the last two or three years, and some of you have even said: 'you have done a lot, now we must carry out, implement what you have decided.' so we must not create new instruments, as some of you have said, before the instruments we have are put to use. and at the same time, we're told, "we have to act now". what we did was -- i've said this many times -- unimaginable a year ago, two years ago. in fact, as far as economic and monetary union is concerned, i have to say - and you have also been able to say this thanks to mrs thyssen's excellent report - that we have already made great progress in economic union and budgetary union and banking union itself. so we have to do more work, but we have come a long way. but what has been underestimated - and i think we all have to admit this, both at the level of the member states and in all the institutions - is the extent, the depth, the gravity of the crisis in some of our countries. the loss of competitiveness, the loss of debt sustainability, has been so great that it takes much longer to rectify the situation. we must not imagine for a moment that with a deficit of 15%, as in a country with a debt of 160%, we can escape austerity policy. we cannot believe that with 10 to 15% deficits on the current account of the balance of payments in other countries, we are escaping a corrective policy, a corrective policy, an austerity policy. it is not imposed by the european institutions in the first place, it is imposed by the facts, it is the consequence of bad governance in a number of countries for a period of time. and action was not taken in time, either at the level of the member states or at the collective level of the european union. so the so-called austerity policy is not a policy that was imposed from above, it is a policy that was inevitable, totally inevitable. the scale and gravity of the crisis have been underestimated. and we've done -- and it's also an underestimation -- a lot of things in the last two, three years. and it's not over yet. that is why the four presidents of the institutions are working on more initiatives. and one of the key points, where there was a lack, was that financial integration was not sufficient. at the beginning of the financial crisis, we set up three or four institutions, of course, but it was mostly at the level of coordination. now we need institutions that are much more european, that are much more common to everyone. in fact, what we're going to do now, with the single supervision, is kind of an unprecedented breakthrough. of course, we have to work hard. we made that decision in june. the commission launched its proposal in september, but the european council in october set out broad guidelines for ministers to complete the work. you also have your share of responsibility in implementing this banking union and we hope - this is an extremely ambitious timetable - to continue and complete the work by the end of the year. once the legislative framework is decided, there will be all the operational work of setting up this single supervisor by the central bank, and this will also take a number of months. so step by step, but in a serious and constant way, we are also working to make this first pillar of the banking union a truly european pillar. we were told, "we must act". we act, but we act in a serious way, in a way that guarantees a certain quality. if there is a certain slowness, it is partly for this reason. so i would say that we have done a lot of things and we are doing things that are, compared to the past, almost revolutionary when we talk about some of the new institutions that we are going to put in place. are we at the end of the road? no, i'm not going. and that is why we are going to work in the december perspective. my dear colleagues, the work will be much harder than we think, because now, after all that we have done - the six-pack, let us hope that the two-pack will also be implemented, in an expeditious manner - with regard to the stability pact and the growth pact, with all that we have done for the financial assistance mechanisms, with all that we have done in all these areas, we are really touching now the hard core of sovereignty and the hard core of solidarity. the next step will therefore be a qualitative leap. we shall also achieve this step by step, and i can assure you that this debate will not be a simple one, either in this house or in the european council or in other institutions. the more we go on, the more difficult it becomes to continue to make progress without touching certain taboos which are close to the concept of sovereignty and the concept of solidarity. i warn you, between october and december, the work will be very hard and decisions will have to be made that will affect a number of taboos. we have spoken of a fifth building block, but the social dimension must be an integral part of every policy. this is what we have done for the europe 2020 strategy. mr barroso and i have fought to get this fifth objective of fighting poverty through. it wasn't easy; the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud. it was eventually integrated into the europe 2020 strategy. the growth and employment pact is nothing more than a social element. what could be more social than trying to create jobs in extremely difficult circumstances? the social dimension is everywhere. these four building blocks, which i have proposed, are building blocks which are traditional in any economic and monetary union. in fact, no head of state or government of any political party or political orientation has ever questioned them at the european council. we will therefore also be working on this social dimension, as i have just said, in the framework of these four building blocks. it is above all at the level of the member states that social policy is most important. when they make decisions on fiscal consolidation - i was minister of budget for a very long time - they have to assess each measure for its social impact. we need a policy where the strongest shoulders carry the heaviest burdens. that's a general rule. but when it comes to executing it and making it real, it's much more difficult. the social dimension is everywhere, both at the level of the member states and at the level of policies decided at european level. some say, "we must act now for growth". ladies and gentlemen, monetary policy - i do not want to pass judgment - is not restrictive policy when we see that interest rates are, in practice, negative interest rates, that the central bank provides liquidity of 1 000 billion euros at the end of the year. a thousand billion! monetary policy is not restrictive at all. as far as budgetary policy is concerned, are we going to go back to deficit spending? no, you can't! but where possible, we'll soften the trajectory. we have done it for spain, for portugal and we may do it for other countries too. in fact, if we do not continue this effort to consolidate the budget, unfortunately the markets will immediately punish these countries. whatever gains are made by widening the path, they are immediately lost by higher interest rates. so there are not a hundred ways to create economic growth in the very short term. the most important thing in the short term is to restore confidence in the euro area. that's the most important thing. i am sorry to quote some figures, but when we say something positive, we must be careful, because it is not even politically correct to say it. when i look at the differential between the 10-year bond rates in most of our countries and the benchmark which is the german interest rate, when i look at the current situation and compare it with the worst situation of the last 12 months, i see a decrease. in italy, it is 239 points, in spain 249 points, in portugal 800 points, in ireland 900 points and in belgium 270 points. so it's not serious to say that there's no result. i hope that these results will be lasting and the hard lesson to be learned from this is, of course, that in the past we have been wrong many times. maybe i'm the only one who's wrong and some of you are always right! i could be wrong. this result is also a clear sign that we can make progress in restoring trust. if this trend is sustained, consumer and business confidence will also increase, and this will create - and we are already seeing early signs of this - a still too modest recovery in growth in late 2012 and early 2013. restoring confidence in the future of the euro area is the best thing we can do for economic growth in the very short term. it is a work that is being done here, and also in the european council, in a gradual, step-by-step approach. but what we do every time is compare the never-before-seen with the past. we will continue the work in december. in the meantime, i hope that we will reach agreement on the multiannual budget in november, but to do that we need something without which no agreement is possible: the sense of compromise and the political will to find it. without this, no agreement is possible. for our part, as in the other councils i have chaired, we will do everything to find a compromise. (applause) i would like to thank
in writing. most of the issues that were discussed at the summit were also discussed at the previous summit. the new thing is that president van rompuy is proposing a separate budget for the eurozone. i understand there's a great need for more money to save banks. but is not the emergency fund the instrument for that? there is no support for a higher emergency fund, but is a new budget not a way of deceiving the citizen? in van rompuy's proposal, a separate budget for the eurozone amounts to an ever-increasing europeanisation of social policy. the call for a greater europe in a time of crisis threatens to undermine subsidiarity in economic, social and fiscal policy. does the council recognize that there is a tension field here? how does he ensure that subsidiarity is not compromised? i would like to warn against these proposals for economic and budgetary union because i am very concerned about them.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. first, the amounts must be much lower. let the eu concentrate on its core tasks. a good suggestion by the way from president schulz, in the dutch press last week, although i disagree with his call for centralisation at european level. another way of saving well is to use the structural funds exclusively for poor regions in the member states with the lowest incomes. finally, the eu's own resources. the european parliament wants the financial transaction tax and the vat surcharge to finance the eu. this is in place of the financial contributions from the member states. that's not desirable. member states are less wasteful, as shown by the 2013 eu budget. the member states have the power to levy taxes and should continue to do so. the union is at the service of the member states, even if it does not always seem so in these economically difficult and tense times.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, human rights are essential for this parliament and for the eu. this is the essence of our values and is also reflected in our trade policies, where we find conditionalities for respect for human rights in relation to all countries across the world with which we have trade relations. this also applies to israel in the context of the association agreement. the vote today concerns the protocol that stems from that association agreement and deals with the pharmaceuticals sector, and with technical requirements and mutual recognition of quality standards. as such, it should be seen as a technical dossier. but, of course, meps on both the committee on foreign affairs and the committee on international trade believed it was important to seek legal guarantees, because we do not want to, directly or indirectly, allow the territorial jurisdiction of israeli authorities over the occupied territories. nor do we want to allow products from the settlements to enter the eu market. we sought legal certainty and we believe that we have acquired the requisite assurances now. we also, as members of this parliament, attach a statement where we reiterate how important we think it is that israel respect human rights. this is not only important for us and for the palestinian people but, most of all, for israel’s future. the israeli government is actually on a path to self-isolation which is not working, making bold statements that the eu should solve its own problems before addressing others. in fact, it is important that the eu show its strength and its muscle, and this protocol does not provide a very effective tool for that purpose. if we want to address human rights violations in israel – and i think we should – we should do so on the basis of the conditionalities provided for under the association agreement. blue-card answer. – mr murphy, as i mentioned, human rights are essential for the european union. i work on human rights on a daily basis and it is a key driver for the work that i do here at parliament. to prepare myself for the vote today and for the text and the work on this protocol, i have spoken to a number of organisations, representing mostly those who care about the human rights of palestinians. we have to ask ourselves whether rejecting this protocol would actually improve the lives of palestinians – and i do not think it will. so i think we must actually use the tools that we have which are stronger – the conditionality agreements that we find in the association agreement with israel – and take this up onto the level that gives us the basis to address matters. the credibility of this parliament is important in order for it to be a broker of real solutions in the middle east peace conflict, and bending the procedures in utterly creative ways actually harms the credibility of this parliament and will not do us any good when seeking solutions that work for the palestinian people. today we are discussing a wonderful protocol to the eu-israel cooperation agreement. israel is a technological country with a great industrial structure which makes it the technological pearl of the middle east. it is the pearl of the middle east in more ways than one. it is fully comparable with western countries in terms of certification and standardisation. this is why this protocol is of enormous importance to the eu. the enormous potential of high-quality products from israel is thus more readily available to european citizens. it is not for nothing that all the computers in the world have something israeli in them and that keeping in touch online would never have happened without the israeli computer industry. another reason for the development of trade relations with the jewish state of israel is, of course, its unremitting fight against terrorism in the gaza strip and terrorism in israel itself. qassam rockets are fired daily at israeli civilians and children and women, and in judea and samaria jewish residents are torched with incendiary bombs. i would also like to ask the commission to recognise jerusalem as the sole and undivided capital of the jewish state of israel and to ask the commission to establish the eu embassy where it belongs, in jerusalem, which is and must remain israeli from east to west.
the european union's external action must be based on international law and in line with the standards and values it promotes. this should certainly also apply to bilateral relations with an important partner such as israel. the committee on foreign affairs has put forward three points. firstly, the israeli responsible authority has no jurisdiction in the territories occupied since 1967. secondly, no industrial product produced in israeli settlements in the west bank and east jerusalem may be certified under the provisions of the protocol. thirdly, palestinian products from the occupied palestinian territories must be treated equally by the israeli authorities. we had a debate with commissioner de gucht in july and he said quite literally: "what i have been doing is giving you the assurances that in good faith i can give you. they are not watertight". i would like to have those watertight guarantees, mr president, and i would therefore like to support something that is requested in the protocol. but at a time when there is an amendment to a protocol on which we can only vote yes or no, i have a problem. i would therefore like the council to assume the same responsibility as commissioner de gucht. i do not know how that is to be done if i have to vote for an amendment, whereas in a protocol we do not have amendments, but only a simple yes or no. if you can give me those guarantees, i know exactly how to behave in the mood.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, in the discussion around dual-use items i would like to focus on technologies. technologies play an ever more important role in the lives of citizens, empowering the individual, breaking monopolies on information and power, and offering plenty of economic, social, cultural and other opportunities for development. technologies also impact human rights. people in egypt, tunisia, iran, syria, china, burma and many other countries, through the use of smart phones and internet access, are able to claim fundamental human rights such as free expression, press freedom, free assembly and access to information, and they can share documented human rights abuses. but those who fear losing control are striking back, increasingly repressing people with the help of technologies as well. prisons all over the world are populated by dissidents, human rights defenders, journalists and citizens whose laptops, mobile phones, social media and e-mail accounts have been compromised. in their cases, mass surveillance, mass censorship, tracking and tracing are not issues of statistics, mr caspary, but of life and death. too often, the repressive technologies that are used to repress people have a label: ‘made in europe’. it is also important that we do not export technologies that can be used to attack the eu itself. speed is indeed important, and we must stop the digital arms trade with more transparency and accountability, a level playing field in europe and more flexibility for updates, so that the eu can play a leading role in an ever-changing world.
- a writer. - the greens have a very simple question to ask you, mrs malmström. when can we expect the revision of the data retention directive? and the answer from you could actually be very simple: "very quickly, mrs sargentini, because the european parliament has been waiting for it for some time". i am afraid you are not going to give that answer, and you seem to be putting off the revision of the directive a little. the reason for this is quite crude, i think, because the member states cannot prove that the data retention directive is useful, but they are also not willing to give up the possibility of storing our telecommunications and internet data. they want to keep that right. however, no european law violates privacy in such a gross way as this law, which keeps information of europeans without suspicion, without judicial approval and without demonstrable result. and while you are delaying the revision of the retention obligation, you are dragging germany before the european court of justice for refusing to comply with that law. but if no one can prove that it works, it's time to abolish the traffic data retention directive and replace it with an alternative, for example, quick freeze, which a judge will decide and where we capture and store a piece of information, targeted at a particular suspect. targeted investigation, judicial review, and no unnecessary invasion of privacy. so, ms. malmström, again, please take that confrontation with those member states and know that we have seen in acta that there are a lot of people in europe who care about their privacy, who want to use that activism and can help you to change those governments' minds.

on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, this directive has been wrong from the very start. let us go back in history and recall that the only reason that this house got to vote on the proposal for data retention was that the member states could not agree. they had no unanimity, remember? there were many member states which did not want this. that is why we got to vote on it, that was the only reason. until today they have been trying to avoid parliamentary intervention. secondly, does it serve a purpose? is it useful? have we become safer since 2005 and 2006? despite the fact that the evaluation only came through after a long delay, there is not a shred of evidence – not a shred – that it contributes to our safety. all the statements about the necessity of this directive are based on assumptions and nothing else. we have yet to see any figures. does it serve the purpose of harmonisation? some colleagues have pointed out that it does not. it does not. it was not meant to from the start, because the member states chose to compensate, or not, companies for the costs that they incurred. it was then deemed a violation of the constitution in many countries. some colleagues may argue about this, but it is a technical point: only the implementation law was deemed unconstitutional. it seems almost impossible to find a way to implement it in a way that is reconcilable with the constitution. in my own country, a report showed that there is widespread abuse and that data are being consulted much more often by many more people than is allowed. does anybody take action? no. so here is a law that was pushed through, that does not serve a purpose, that seems to be a violation of the constitution and of which there is widespread abuse. so of course the commission and the member states say ‘great law, let’s have more of it’. i say to the commissioner: we should not wait for a review. it should be repealed today.
in these troubling financial and economic times for the european union, the prospect of a promising impetus is certainly very welcome. it is also a reference for the forthcoming eu-japan trade negotiations. the draft resolution makes very clear in paragraphs 2 and 3 the importance and potential for the eu of deepening trade relations with the major global economy of japan. at the same time, the motion for a resolution identifies in a very realistic way the major obstacles to a free trade agreement between the eu and japan. on the japanese side, these are mainly the so-called ntbs, non-tariff barriers and barriers to market access in public procurement. the european parliament's willingness to keep a tight rein on the commission's negotiating process shows commitment and responsibility, because what counts in the end is the positive outcome for our citizens. i wish the commissioner every success in the negotiations ahead.
i would like to thank mrs regina bastos for the excellent cooperation which enabled me to vote in favour of her report. i think it is right that we are celebrating 20 years of the single market, because it has created more jobs and more economic growth. but there are also what i would call a number of fringes on which i have questions to ask commissioner barnier in particular. first of all, the increase in scale, which is an automatic consequence of the internal market. sometimes that's good and efficient, but it also has cultural effects. in the old days, if you went to another city, you would see other stores with products you didn't know. these days, things are starting to look more and more alike. a lot of people complain about that, too. they want fewer european chains and more small, independent businesses making and selling handicrafts or other products that you don't see anywhere else. does the commission agree with me that we should support smes, irrespective of whether companies have plans to expand their business to other european member states? is there not a need to support small, independent businesses and companies which want to maintain their own character and remain small? is it not discriminatory if european funds are only available to companies that do want to cross the border? i agree with the commissioner, but also with the rapporteur, that the single market is a great success. however, i still hear a lot of complaints about the transposition of directives, especially from smes, and i ask you again to focus more on regulations so that the legislation is the same for the whole of europe. i agree with colleagues, including mr schwab, that the celebrations of the 20th anniversary of the single market were very successful, but in my view they were still too much inward-looking and the citizens of europe still know too little about the success of the single market. europe is less popular now than it was 20 years ago. communication, or marketing, as we call it, is our biggest problem. a customer - as an sme would say - needs to know that a product is very, very good before he buys it, and that applies to europe as well. the single market must create the conditions for people to get the best out of themselves, but they must be aware of this. that is why i am calling on you, commissioner, to set up a great marketing programme for 2017 so that all citizens of europe can see the benefits.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on employment and social affairs. − mr president, i think the second semester was an improvement on the first. when i started working as a rapporteur on the semester, around this time last year, there was hardly any debate at all about job creation or recovery. it was all financial figures and economic variables then. this has changed for the better, but although the debate seems to have shifted in the right direction, the actions have not yet shifted. while the leaders of the member states cheerfully announced in january that they would all come up with job plans, none of them did. while lip service is paid to alleviating poverty, the number of poor in europe has risen sharply. and while everybody expresses their grave and graver concern over rising youth unemployment, they stop short of a binding youth guarantee. for the next semester, in 2013, i want to see the commission and the council address rising unemployment and rising poverty much more seriously: for instance, through that binding youth guarantee; through the assessment of economic measures for their effect on the poorest and most vulnerable people; through an approach to wages that addresses them not merely as an economic variable, but as the money out of which people pay for their food and the roof over their heads. i deplore the fact that, although the semester is becoming more and more important, it is not becoming more democratic. at the moment, we as a parliament can do no more than give friendly advice. this makes it particularly vital that there should be a strong majority for this report tomorrow if we want our advice to be heard, because only with a strong majority will there be a moral incentive for the commission and the council to take the recommendations on board. one sentence threatens that majority. it is in paragraph 23 on the fiscal compact. i would appeal strongly to colleagues on the left and the right in this parliament to vote in favour of the identical amendments 1 and 14 to change this sentence, regardless of your opinion on the fiscal compact, because this report is not about that. this report is not the place to settle our differences on the fiscal compact, it is about the european semester. the text of the amendments neither applauds nor criticises the compact and it should be capable of uniting us as a parliament. i especially appeal to my fellow rapporteur, mr gauzès to please not allow this sentence, which is unnecessary for his report, and not to poison the otherwise strong and good message we are sending out.
last july, the member states received a set of recommendations to bring their national finances into order and to keep them in order. hopefully these recommendations will convince european governments to implement the necessary reforms. the financial and economic situation in europe is very serious. the figures on the growing debt burden and the lack of competitiveness in significant parts of europe speak for themselves. it is important to emphasize that this is a package of recommendations and not of commitments. we have already made agreements in europe in the stability and growth pact on debt reduction and medium-term objectives. the member states themselves shall determine how those objectives are to be achieved. and national parliaments ensure democratic control over specific measures. as much as some here would like to take over this role of the national parliaments, the european parliament cannot fulfil that role. it lacks the legitimacy and competence to do so. as far as my country, the netherlands, is concerned, the negotiators for the new government are doing well to follow up the recommendations of the council which came in july. the accelerated raising of the retirement age to 67 and the limitation of the mortgage interest deduction are urgently needed to keep public finances sustainable in the long term. i really hope that mr rutte and mr samson will make these choices.
the commission has sent member states a binding list of socioeconomic measures. the commission considers that these are the priority areas. the commission is now in charge in the member states, thanks in part to the european parliament. but everyone can see in his own country that unemployment is too high, or that public deficits are too large, or that regulations are too onerous. we do not need the commission for that. mr president, it seems impossible to have a single monetary union for economies with unequal speeds. the eurozone is permanently unstable. but the north does not want to continue to pay for the south and the south does not want to be commanded by the commission. what the commission should propose is to dissolve the eurozone, because the south urgently needs to be freed from the grip of the euro. mr president, when will we receive from the commission the plan to abolish the eurozone in its present form? that is the highest priority.
the european parliament has a duty to ensure that the european union is not left behind. there is also a mutual dependence between the european union and russia, especially in the field of trade and energy. but these trade relations - the commissioner has just said this - are certainly not optimal. the reason for this is also that russia has so far applied only its own rules, and this is often a source of great frustration for european industry. i have seen today that in the business ranking published by the world bank, russia is only in 112th place. that's because russia is a very difficult country to do business with. that is precisely why russia's accession to the wto is a very good development, one which we in the ppe group also welcome and which we want to see enshrined in a resolution. but, mr president, we must also check that all the agreements made are being respected. let me make two further comments. if we want to exploit the full potential of economic relations with russia, we must ensure that russia also fulfils all its obligations under the wto. in the run-up to accession, there are still many issues which concern me, such as protectionist measures, unilateral temporary tariff increases, the production and sale of counterfeit goods. these cases have now been named and must be closely monitored. the accession to the wto is an incredibly important step, but there are other specific issues that the eu and russia need to settle bilaterally. these matters will be discussed in the swoboda report, in which we should, as today, once again call the attention of the services concerned to ensure that the negotiations, including those on a partnership agreement, are simply being resumed smoothly.
on behalf of the alde group, i would like to thank commissioner füle for his excellent contribution this morning. it is not surprising that the parliamentary elections of 23 september again failed to meet international standards. because let's face it, as long as lukashenko rules belarus with an iron fist, nothing will change. elections without fair competition are not elections. they are nothing but a farce. the result is a parliament in which there is now literally no opposition member left. there is a tendency to accept the isolation of belarus, but that would be a totally wrong indifference. we must not lose faith in change and must continue to work for the opening up of belarus. i am glad that commissioner füle has shown this morning that he shares this view. sanctions only work in part. the pressure will really have to come from below. the belarusians themselves must long for a different government, a different life. there are democratic movements in belarus. they need to continue to grow and they need our support very badly. i therefore call on commissioner füle to continue to work on strengthening civil society in belarus in the context of the new neighbourhood policy. the eu also has to step up pressure on the belarusian regime to simplify and make visa requirements affordable for belarusians. it's a way for us to exchange more. a commission proposal is on the table, but minsk refuses to sign. the european union is not only a political force, but also a political force. the country did not join the customs union with russia and kazakhstan for nothing. it depends on investments and loans from russia and increasingly also from china. these countries are not asking any questions about the human rights situation in belarus. we must continue to do so and in particular support the belarusians themselves.
for the epp group the official turnout in the latest parliamentary elections in belarus at the end of september was 74%. laughably, the belarusian historian and publicist valeri karbalevich has delivered a devastating judgment. independent observers and voters saw through numerous half-empty polling stations all day on september 23, a resounding victory for the lukashenko regime. the repressive regime in minsk thus reaped what it sowed. if you systematically simulate elections, you shouldn't be surprised if civil society reacts in the same way. the vote on the resolution was taken by a large majority, with the majority of the votes going to the opposition. this should give much thought to the reformist elites in the country. the council and the commission would therefore do well to invest time and energy in patiently establishing relations with these belarusian elites at this time. combine principles with change, in german: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the this is of great benefit to both belarus and the eu, and i have every confidence that commissioner füle will be thinking and working on this.
i would like to express the wish of my colleague, mr nicholson, that malala yousafzai should make a full recovery from her serious injuries. i hear that recovery is progressing, too. her deeply sad, yes, horrifying case leads to two depressing observations about the pakistani education system. poverty forces many parents to send their children to madrassas, where education is free. and it is precisely in these madrassas that the subject of islam, islamic studies, is mandated by the state, with the result of promoting intolerant thoughts, not only towards religious minorities, but also towards women and, respectively, towards education for girls. in short, the real problem in pakistan is the grossly underfunded education system itself. significantly, malala comes from the swat valley, where only one in three girls goes to school. the pakistani authorities must not be left behind by her unimaginable courage. and that must be the urgent message from brussels to islamabad.

- i'm the reporter. - the commission's proposal on this subject was made long before the events in norway, which have once again put us face to face with the facts. it is necessary to have as uniform rules as possible in a common market in the european union in order to restrict as much as possible the sale of materials which can be used to make explosives and to exclude as many risks as possible. it is therefore good that the commission has proposed a regulation and not a directive. we believe that as many countries as possible should comply with the common rules. there's been an exception. parliament and the commission were almost in complete agreement, but the council was not in complete agreement. there is one country that already has a registration system instead of a licensing system, and wanted to continue with it. parliament has agreed to this and we are satisfied with the compromise. in five years' time, we will evaluate which system is the best, and perhaps we can then have a uniform system throughout the european union. the decision now taken concerns a first list of certain products with a certain concentration which can only be sold under licence. there's a second list of products with a rule that says if there's anything suspicious, it should be reported as soon as possible. it is therefore also important that national contact points are set up in each country where any suspicious transaction can be recorded, so that the governments of those countries and the commission can react as quickly as possible. i think that parliament's proposals on the definition of a suspicious transaction have improved the regulation considerably. finally, we must be constantly vigilant, and it is also good that the regulation includes the possibility of consultation between member states, with member states and with experts in this field. if there is a need to change the list - and this was an important concession by parliament, especially with regard to the first list - if there is a need to add a new product or to withdraw a product, then this must be done through a legislative procedure. the parliament and the commission would have preferred that this should have been done through the so-called delegated acts, but you cannot have everything, and so parliament agreed that the council should have its way on this point. as regards changes in concentrations, the commission can still decide on them by means of delegated acts and then parliament and the council can finally decide on them within a certain period: the commission shall adopt the draft budget in accordance with the procedure referred to in article 4 (2). there's been a lot of talk about ammonium nitrate: we agree that it is covered by the reach regulation so far, but also that the evaluation which will take place in due course will look at whether it is not better to bring this substance under the current regulation. finally, the approval or monitoring of what we have agreed must be done by the member states. we assume that the fines to be imposed in the various member states for infringement of this regulation will be adequate. we hope that this regulation will be a step towards preventing terrorist attacks and other crimes in the european union.

- i'm the reporter. - good evening mr. tajani and all the people who are helping you. mr president, we have here a fine technical and long-standing dossier of great importance for the internal market. it is not just the technical requirements for the two-wheelers that are at issue, but also the supervision. the first is the technical requirements: we have had five or so points of discussion over the last two years, and these are the subjects of this regulation. firstly, the categorisation of vehicles will become clearer. i have spoken at length with mr panzeri about the so-called atvs, and we have settled that matter in a proper way. we have been very vocal about the abs and cbs braking systems on the powered two-wheelers, and the braking systems should make the job safer. we agreed that there would be a further study to determine whether the small scooters were effective. we have also looked at the emission requirements of the scooters, because they are quite polluting, especially in urban areas. we looked at the sustainability tests: is a scooter as clean after five years as it was after the first? and we also looked at the lights on those vehicles. but, mr president, market surveillance is also very important to keep cheap, unsafe goods - and they come from china, india and korea - out of the eu. this has nothing to do with protectionism, but everything to do with the quality of protection for our citizens. before i go any further, i would like to thank once again all those involved in this consultation, in particular the danish presidency, which has done a great deal on the dossier, but also, of course, the council and the commission, for what we have all achieved. a few more questions for the commissioner. first of all, the negotiations on the delegated and implementing acts. we have agreed that the industry will have enough time to introduce this new regulation, but if the negotiations on the delegated and implementing acts take too long, then there will be no lead time left. so i would like the commissioner to give me his assurance that the commission will proceed with the implementing and delegated acts. a second point, mr president, concerns the emission requirements, and in particular euro 5 in 2016. the commission will investigate this and the intention is that we will look not only at pollution but also at costs and, again, at lead time. in recent days, there has been some confusion about electric bicycles. electric bicycles are not covered by this directive. they 're just covered by the machinery directive: 250 watts and a top speed of 15 miles. and i'd like to keep it that way, because more powerful electric bikes only lead to major accidents. if the manufacturers of electric bicycles want something different, they can do it in this directive. finally, mr president, the contact with the british motorcyclists. the british motorcyclists have been very critical of us, and rightly so. it is good for democracy if the british motorcyclists are critical of us. modify is allowed, perform is not. and that's something the british motorcyclists should be able to understand.
i would also like to thank mr wim van de camp and mr antonio panzeri for their cooperation. these are two important regulations to improve the internal market. there will be more safety, a better environment, more clarity for producers and consumers, less costs, especially administrative burdens, and above all less regulation. as a liberal, i'm very much in favor of that. it was a very technical file, very technical. and i often felt like a technocratic. it became so difficult and the number of amendments was so enormous that i felt more like a mechanic than a politician. i would have liked to have had a little more of a political dimension in this report, mr president. for example, do we want interchangeable batteries in the future when the vehicles are electric? we want to encourage innovation, or do we already have everything in the form of type approval? do we want more visibility, for example on the side, to ensure safety? how can we prevent counterfeit products or bad products? mr van de camp has already referred to this. for example, do we want to put "made in europe" on our vehicles, or do we leave them in the middle where they are made? how can we avoid unfair competition? i often hear complaints, for example, that tractors with trailers are used, while on the other hand trucks are used which have to meet much more complex type-approval requirements. and last but not least, mr president, the driver often determines how safe the vehicle behaves. i would like to see us establish minimum european rules for drivers and provisions on regular updating or information, because you will get your driving licence someday, but the technical changes in vehicles are changing too and drivers are often not prepared for that. all in all, mr president, it is a good result. i am satisfied with it and will advise my group to vote in favour. but in the future i would like to see us make cases like this a little more political, so that the citizens outside this building are also a little more interested in our work. then we can expect political fireworks. i hope that works. i thank you very much, and i think that there will be a very large majority in favour of these two dossiers tomorrow.

on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, first of all i would like to express my gratitude to the rapporteur, who did a magnificent job. i would like to start by addressing the so-called fourth pillar, democratic legitimacy, because i think that this is an issue which is under-exposed. in my view we do not only have an economic crisis, a banking crisis and a debt crisis; we have a democratic crisis, and the issue of democratic legitimacy is not a nice little extra that we can do once the economic crisis is over. no, democratic legitimacy is a precondition for solving the situation we are in. in this respect i have to say that i am extremely worried when i see that the european union has taken major steps ahead over the last three years, but in an increasingly undemocratic, non-transparent way; increasingly intergovernmental and increasingly outside any treaty structures. it is not even intergovernmental anymore. that is not the way to go. if we want to solve the situation, if we want to make that leap ahead into the future, then the democratic crisis is the first thing that we need to solve and i think there are some interesting elements in the report. i think one of the things that we need to look at very closely is individual accountability of european commissioners, accountability before this parliament. a second issue that we need to look at again – which is not a nice luxury extra – is transparency. in a democratic political union, transparency is key to accountability. so far we have worked under the diplomatic method with the tradition of diplomatic discretion, but now we need transparency, we need openness, we need to enable citizens to watch what we are doing. parliament, too, should look very closely at the way it organises its work. we should enable ourselves to exercise meaningful parliamentary scrutiny, and i would like to add here as well that although parliament should reflect the cultural diversity of europe, we should not actually be speaking on behalf of the member states. we are here to defend the general european interest and as i listen to the debates here i occasionally detect hints of nationalism. finally about banking supervision: i would still like to emphasise that although there is a need to move quickly to set up the banking union, we all know, as we are working on the proposals, that they are not sufficient in the long term. i think that we need, now already, to have a long-term perspective, because we know that the ecb cannot cover everything. it cannot cover the non-eurozone countries; it cannot cover the non-banking sector; and i have too my question marks over the concentration of power. so i do think that we need a roadmap towards adequate and democratic banking supervision. fifteen years ago, mr president, the member states were living in prosperity, peace and mutual understanding. now we have growing poverty, riots, and empty promises. everyone outside brussels understands that europe has taken the wrong turn. but this parliament wants to continue on the path it has taken. parliament wants a genuine monetary union in which the budgetary rights of the member states and thus their sovereignty disappear. and a banking union which will open the way for the esm to recapitalise failing banks. supervised by brussels, which a year ago called dexia an excellent bank! and finally, a transfer union in which the northern taxpayers will have to pay for the rest of eternity not only for their own social system but also for that of other countries. and everyone will be poorer. president, sovereignty is something to be proud of, not a historical mistake. if this report is adopted, it is because, as is the socialist custom, there are more beneficiaries than payers. but i will vote against it. we must strive for prosperity for all, not for redistribution of poverty.
mr president, the thyssen report does not put an end to the budgetary fetishism which is leading europe straight to the abyss. in southern europe, economies are shrinking more and more, but also in the netherlands, the economy shrank by 1.1% in the last quarter. the fact that the conservative-liberal vvd is pursuing an irresponsible policy of austerity is linked to their ideology. they want an ever smaller government anyway and see social rights as an obstacle to competitiveness. but the fact that the social democratic party is cooperating in this is completely incomprehensible. before the election, their leader samsom had already renounced the 3% norm. now he's helping to implement dramatic cuts, while the imf is encouraging countries like the netherlands to invest and boost consumer confidence. i call on the social democrats to distance themselves from the policy of their dutch colleagues and to vote in favour of our amendments, in which we consider the current special economic conditions to be sufficient grounds for a prudent and therefore flexible application of the 3% rule. this is the only way to get europe's economies back on their feet.
the thyssen report, mr president, seems to point to a genuine economic and monetary union, but it forgets one important economic lesson: a monetary union can only exist between countries which have enough in common. that is the common denominator, mr president, that is missing in the eurozone. for example, labour mobility is low and unit labour costs vary enormously. this week's edition of the economist shows that france's competitiveness is a very big risk. with greece and spain, france could soon become the sick man of europe. the many proposals in the thyssen report are therefore far from being realistic. in the end, the bretton woods system could not survive either. it was only when the link to the gold standard was loosened that new opportunities for the global economy were opened up. the present eurozone cannot continue unchanged either. only after the zone has been tackled will a genuine economic and monetary union be possible. if this approach is not followed, the faucet will remain open.

in writing. − i am of the opinion that children should be protected and crime should be tackled through law enforcement. however, the report’s focus on government campaigns and the extension of enforcement to isps and other self-regulating mechanisms is setting the wrong priorities and risks privatising responsibilities that lie in the hands of states, while diminishing the role of parents in their children’s education. measures included in the report furthermore demonstrate an unwarranted bias towards the perceived dangers of the internet, limiting the opportunities for education and innovation. rather, i prefer to strengthen young people’s resilience and independence. efforts should be directed at educating children and young people, with the help of their parents and teachers, to take ownership of their actions on the web and to develop e-skills. i recognise that advancements in ict can raise social capital, facilitating fundamental rights such as freedom of expression and access to information. i consider the participation of children and youth in today’s digital society an essential aspect of their development. while fully recognising the need for effective law enforcement, particularly the need to end serious crimes such as child pornography, i regret the restrictive nature of the proposed measures prevents me from giving my support.

on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, we have heard a lot about rules. i want to say a bit about the gist of the debate on this resolution and the context in which it is taking place. the internet and technologies have changed people’s lives around the world, empowering them to access information, to express themselves freely, to assemble and to document human rights violations, for example; and for businesses and entrepreneurs, too, the opportunities are very exciting. with the help of technology, it is easier to challenge monopolies, including powers that have traditionally lain in the hands of government and media. at the same time we have to be careful that no new monopolies emerge. revolutionary change never comes without protest from those with a vested interest: businesses that want to secure their profitable models; governments that want to control people. multinational organisations are the new arena for battles of this kind. the international telecommunications union may have been a technical and somewhat obscure organisation dealing with standards, but these standards are increasingly being used in political fights and in the re-territorialisation of the internet in the grip of nation states, and the standards are also being tweaked to protect business models. under the guise of fighting spam, speech has been stifled; and, in the name of cyber security, online surveillance has dramatically increased. banning ‘voice over internet protocol’ services hampers free speech by criminalising it, as in africa, or throttling it, as was the case in the netherlands – which is why my party pushed for enshrining net neutrality in law. our resolution addresses some of the most urgent concerns relating to internet governance. some argue that this is actually a contradiction in terms, but what is certainly true is that the internet has developed into the fruitful platform it is today without much regulation. today we are talking specifically about the international telecommunications union (itu), which will host the world conference on international telecommunications in dubai, and it makes sense to update the system of regulation because this has not been done for a very long time. however, we should bear in mind that the itu’s recommendations are non-binding documents that promote best practices. each of the 27 eu member states has a vote, and if the eu wants to be a global player – if we want our voice to be heard – we must speak out loudly as one, and we should further the development of the internet as a truly public sphere in which fundamental freedoms are guaranteed.

europe is facing a huge economic crisis. member states are being injected with money, citizens are being squeezed out. how is it then that the european union is writing cheques to countries thousands of kilometres away? the eu can't even keep its own pants on, so why is it giving away billions? since the start of the macrofinancial assistance scheme, over 7 billion has been disbursed. much of it is given away as gifts, often to countries with a reputation for bribery, corruption and organized crime. this time kyrgyzstan is the lucky recipient of european money. macro-financial assistance is supposedly intended for countries neighbouring the european union. kyrgyzstan is in central asia, more than six thousand kilometers from here. that's not a neighboring country of europe, it's a country next to china. is that already within brussels' sphere of influence? where does this end? human rights violations are taking place in kyrgyzstan, and many activists are behind bars. it is also one of the most corrupt countries in the world. and the european union is now entrusting this country with 30 million euros, 15 million of which as a gift to be spent freely. please, have fun with it, says the european union! the contrast with the situation in europe cannot be greater. europe is on the verge of social and economic collapse, but the eu continues to spend money as if nothing is wrong. the taxpayer can take the fall for this. the hard-earned money of the citizens is being taken away, because the eu wants to give financial gifts to verwegistan. mr president, this policy is a flagrant insult. the european union must stop this.
rapporteur. − madam president, this report is mainly directed at high representative ashton and her external action service. technological developments have a revolutionary impact on the lives of people all over the world. several eu member states, and the united nations, have now identified access to the internet as an enabler of fundamental rights, and the commission agrees that digital freedoms are part of the copenhagen criteria. initial policy steps through the european instrument for democracy and human rights and the no disconnect strategy are welcome, but we need to be much more ambitious. tomorrow, we vote for the first strategy to mainstream technologies into the eu’s external actions. i am not suggesting over-regulation – that would hurt the open internet – but in some policy areas rules are needed and need to be updated to match the revolutionary impact of technological developments with adequate democratic oversight. the commission is working on a cyber security plan which this parliament is eagerly awaiting, but let us ensure that there is no zero-sum game between cyber security and digital freedoms. we must put people first. the struggle for human rights has a growing technology component: prisons are increasingly populated by dissidents confronted with their own internet and mobile communications having been compromised by the authorities. people facing repression deserve eu support, and in any case should not be targeted with tools and technologies developed and exported from within the eu. promoting and defending human rights also means enabling people to circumvent mass censorship and to evade cyber attacks. while the recent eu export bans on repressive technologies to syria and iran are important ad hoc sanctions, generally, more transparency and accountability are needed. technologies, tools or services custom-made for targeted human rights violations should be categorised separately, as single-use technologies, and should be restricted. an eu law for intercept standards without the context of the rule of law loses all meaning. in trade agreements, but also in development assistance, we need to include digital freedoms in conditionalities and to enforce them. there are a lot of opportunities in eu development policies: we can bridge the digital divide, build and install basic ict infrastructures, provide people with access to knowledge and information, and enable online education in remote areas. in the first critical hours after natural disasters or during humanitarian crises, ad hoc emergency mobile and internet connections should be set up. icts are also essential for effective citizen election monitoring. in turning these opportunities into actions we must be vigilant and recall that the eu cannot credibly promote or protect digital freedoms in the world if they are not safeguarded at home. european companies should be reminded of their corporate social responsibilities. we need human rights impact assessments in the r&d phase and must implement the concept of human rights by design. the external action service should take the lead in globally promoting and protecting digital freedoms by synergising trade, security and foreign policies, and by aligning our eu values and interests. we need to do justice to the new reality that technological developments create and update our laws and regulations to mainstream digital freedom. presidents, governments cannot do this alone and the private sector has increasing responsibilities. the internet is now governed by a so-called multi-stakeholder approach which developed organically into a network of public and private stakeholders. this model can only function when it is inclusive, including small businesses, internet users and consumers, who need a seat at the table. we must consider the internet public and public interests. i would like to thank the shadows who contributed to this and all the stakeholders who gave their input to the discussion paper. this report was crowd-sourced, which is another reminder of how technology can assist democracy, and create more openness and participation within the eu too.
mr president, the unitary patent was a great idea. the automatic granting of patent protection in several countries by standardising and mutually recognising patent issuance. the idea still exists, but the implementation has become so complicated that the actual protection of intellectual property has become uncertain. we have a legal uncertainty situation with four different patents during a transitional period and the length of that transitional period is also unclear. this proposal will only create uncertainty, not protection. it's not cheaper. for inventors, for start-ups, for smes, operating only nationally, the costs are increasing and the new establishments in paris and london will not be free either. the negotiations could have been more fruitful if italy and spain had cooperated and if the billions in aid now being given had not been unreasonable. but above all, is this end result an example of the difficult ratios: a union that is not a union. countries feel unrepresented, citizens feel unrepresented and business no longer feels represented. perhaps, mr president, this union is really only for civil servants and politicians.
first of all, mr president, i would like to thank the rapporteurs for their long and hard work. so here we are. tired of fighting after 40 years. is that commendable? is that enough? is it european enough for you? actually, it's not good at all: in 40 years, more could have been done and we could have achieved much more with a real patent. after 40 years of working on the patent, this is what was created: instead of a crown jewel, a disgrace. because it represents everything that everyone in europe hates: the struggle of languages, the egoism of nations, each for itself. a complex legal system that hardly anyone fully understands. this could have been a fantastic goal for european innovation; i'm still hoping that's what it's gonna be. but then there is more to it than just a good patent, and i hope that there will be a really good eu patent, which is really working on innovation. at the moment, the innovation budget is in danger of being pulverized in the battle over budgets and over historical interests. i really call on everyone to work on real innovation and on sufficient funding for it so that it can become a crown jewel and we can be competitive in europe and in the world.
i would like to thank the rapporteurs for their good work, but i would like to congratulate commissioner barnier on his having done the best he could. after 40 years of debate, you have achieved what you have now, and i commend you on that. i thank you on behalf of my region as well. i 'm from brabant . there's brainport, and there's an average of four patent applications a day. they'll be thrilled to hear this. i was an inventor myself before i got into politics. i have two patents in my name, but unfortunately i only have benelux patents because i could not afford to apply for patents for all of europe. i'm also an intellectual property lawyer, and i know how difficult it is to legally protect your patents. so, congratulations to the commission, congratulations to parliament, for the patent legislation we are adopting today. i do regret very much that two countries are not participating, so that it does not become a community patent, two countries which have, after all, been operating under the same system for almost 40 years. i find it absolutely incomprehensible, particularly since these are countries which are not doing well economically at the moment. i thank you, mr president, and i hope that we shall adopt this by a large majority this afternoon.
