member of the commission. − mr president, there have been many interesting remarks and contributions, and i thank you all very much for this lively debate. it is difficult to comment on all the detailed points raised. my colleagues and i have had the opportunity to meet the committee on transport and members of the european parliament. my colleagues have participated in all these discussions. they can continue to discuss these issues if you decide to continue this discussion. i would like to point out that this is a very modest proposal. this entire package is very modest. it is being attacked from both sides. one side considers that it does not go nearly far enough to improve the competitiveness of european airports and to solve the problem. they argue that you can already see on eurocontrol maps how the centre of europe will be squeezed in the very near future, and there is a real need to do something about this. on the other hand, on the subject of social protection and related issues, i should say that i started these negotiations with a meeting with representatives of trade unions. we managed to form an impression of what a balanced proposal might look like. the provisions which they asked for have been included in this new proposal, which contains more provisions in this area than existing legislation. so i am deeply convinced that this is a balanced and moderate proposal. people ask what real liberalisation is. i can tell you: real liberalisation would go much further. it could mean auctioning off frankfurt airport’s new runway slots, for instance, as called for initially. you can not imagine how many bright ideas people in various camps have. so i am genuinely convinced that this is a modest proposal. i am not insisting on getting this package through at any cost. i am not heavily lobbying for it. it is your decision tomorrow what to do with this package. it is a political decision. you must consider the package as a whole; you cannot take some elements out and leave some elements in. if you take a political decision that this package should not go through then so be it. of course i would like to comment more concretely and in a more detailed manner on several aspects of the package in the course of the present debate, but i am also subject to time limits and i am passionate about keeping to them. however i should just like to refer to the fact that somebody said today i am an advocate of night flights. i cannot understand where that idea came from. yes, of course, mr kohlíček made the very interesting proposal that we could organise these slots, and all air traffic management, in a completely different manner using new technology, including sesar. yes, that would be possible, but member states have opted for another way of gradually improving the functioning of air transport infrastructure and we must follow their decision. so let us see what your decision will be tomorrow. of course, it is clear that if this package does not get political support we will take it back.

for the ppe group. mr president, as has been said, the ppe group believes that legislation on the transport of animals in an ideal world is based on scientific facts, and then the right conditions of transport are often more important or just as important for the welfare of an animal as the duration of transport. but, mr president, we also know that the world is far from ideal. and if you make legislation based on transport conditions, as the council did in 2004, you have to have a final control and sanction regime in place to get the rotten apples out and not punish those who have invested in the right implementation and expensive cars. let that control be once again the achilles heel of this regulation. and as we have heard so many times before, the commission washes its hands of the matter, points out that the member states are responsible for monitoring this legislation, and then the commission backs down again. why, commissioner, do you find that there are great differences in controls between the member states, but have you not increased your own fvo inspections in this area significantly in recent years? why is it that you know in detail how much the penalties differ between member states when it comes to the transport of goods, but you have deliberately decided not to investigate this in animals, in living creatures? i would like to thank the rapporteur for all the work he has done in formulating the european parliament's position on the promotion of animal safety during transport. i am therefore in full agreement with the resolution as it stands. however, if it is said that this resolution is very clear about our support for an eight-hour limit on transport, then i really have a very different text in mind. firstly, this resolution states that some things have not been scientifically proved, and secondly we call on the commissioner to consider doing something about the eight-hour transport time. that's too weak. i therefore put forward an alternative resolution, in which i take over the rapporteur's text in its entirety, except for the two paragraphs which, in a very difficult and vague way, ask for the eight hours of transport to be taken into account. i simply replaced those two paragraphs with a direct request to the commission: the commission has already made a number of proposals, including the introduction of a limit of eight hours for transport, the written declaration signed by a majority of this parliament and the initiative supported by a million citizens. it is a tightening of the rapporteur's text; the rapporteur's report is fine for the rest, but at eight o'clock we as a parliament must insist more strongly and we are doing so with the alternative resolution.
president. - thank you to mr wojciechowski for his excellent work and welcome the new commissioner. i am in favour of transporting animals for slaughter for up to eight hours. i think that the transport of animals for slaughter should really be made more friendly to the animals themselves. at the same time, the commission should look at how the transport of carcases instead of live animals can be promoted. i would rather pay a fair price for my meat than eat cheap meat from animals that have suffered unnecessarily during transport. i think the key to this debate is also enforcement and control. we see in the report that some member states are not sufficiently critical in the control and inspection of meat; there is a lack of enforcement, poor compliance; i think that's very important to address, because we cannot tolerate a deterioration in animal welfare and distortion of competition. it is precisely at this point that i call on the commissioner to make a real effort.
in writing. unnecessary towing of animals should be stopped. this can only be started by an eight-hour limit on all animal transport for slaughter. in 2005, legislation was introduced to improve transport conditions for animals. that has had only a small effect so far. many countries and carriers do not comply with the regulations in force. sufficient room for movement and drinking water, normal temperatures: it's in the law, animals have a right to it. the commission must therefore also ensure that member states and carriers comply with that law. in addition, we can reduce animal suffering by setting an eight-hour limit. an eight-hour limit is easy to control and encourages slaughter in one's own region. so less unnecessary animal suffering. more than 1.1 million eu citizens have also signed up to an 8-hour limit, an unprecedented number. the commission has also proposed that the council should be consulted on the draft budget for the financial year. now we have a chance to show that this wasn't just some nice talk for the stage. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the animals are not left behind.
blue-card question. – ms gál, i hear you say that you are complimenting the rapporteur on an excellent report – you actually pick out a topic, minority rights – and then you go on to say that fundamental rights should not be a political debate, therefore the epp will put forward an alternative resolution. could you please explain to the european citizens and this house why putting forward an alternative resolution dismissing the excellent report by the rapporteur is not a political game with fundamental rights?
i see an interesting interplay between you and your colleague mr rehn and president van rompuy. in september you proposed a 'justice scoreboard', an annual assessment of the legal systems of all member states in the context of the european semester. commissioner rehn took the lead and put more emphasis on the quality and independence of national justice systems in his annual growth analysis last month. the country-specific recommendations that result from this will be given a somewhat more binding character in the plans of mr van rompuy, the third man in your trio. he also called for the commission and the member states to translate the recommendations into reform contracts with financial incentives. it may be a rather difficult construction, but i think that if we were to stipulate in a contract that the shortcomings in legal protection should be addressed, this could be a major step forward in the monitoring of our fundamental rights. may i compliment you on that? but may i ask you to look at not just justice, but also democracy and media freedom and the whole package? i'd like your answer.
mr president, this so-called 'future' of economic and monetary union is in reality the beginning of the end. the end of national sovereignty, democracy and prosperity. the banking union means that in the end the dutch taxpayer pays the bill for bankruptcies in southern europe. eurobonds are going to cost the netherlands billions every year. countries that make fun of it will have to pay the price. the netherlands will bear the costs. in socialist eu parlance this is called solidarity. the eu as a permanent charitable institution is now in full swing. the dutch treasury is being plundered. and as if that were not bad enough, the netherlands is also at the mercy of brussels. the european union will impose and enforce contracts. the labour market, the housing market, taxation, the tentacles of brussels are everywhere. the netherlands loses control over its own budget, which must first be submitted to brussels. the european union's hunger for more money, power and authority is insatiable. if the netherlands does not resist, it will be reduced to a mere province within a federal european superstate, and that is not desirable.
the european parliament has already expressed its views on the commission's proposal for a regulation on the european union's financial and monetary union. you can count on our support. i would like to say to mr verhofstadt and others: complaining will not help, but we must show the strength to restore confidence and provide jobs for our citizens. crucial to restoring trust is that we adopt the two-pack in the short term. your demands are unrealistic. you too must take a step forward and let us show that parliament can also act on this point. european banking supervision is also crucial to restoring confidence. but it is a matter of strong european banking supervision. i am concerned when i see the debate between the ministers, where the national interests seem to prevail. parliament has an ambitious position. we are ready for negotiations and we will make a firm commitment to that.
in writing. − i abstained on amendment 1 because of its reference to smart fiscal consolidation in the framework of the eu’s excessive deficit procedures; however i strongly support the notion that a budgetary freeze would have been more desirable.
in writing. the pvv believes that brussels is taking too much power. the same applies to this case, where the commission is again given all the power. animal welfare is a matter of civility and decency. although the pvv has a very warm heart for animal welfare, we believe that any legislation and enforcement should be regulated at national level, and not dictated by brussels bureaucrats. the pvv calls on all its fellow meps to raise the issue of animal welfare in their home countries, especially where abuses are still taking place.
mr president, the house is full of talk about human rights. lady ashton in particular, always blowing high from the tower. the eu must promote human rights worldwide, she said. and my party also regrets when human rights are violated anywhere in the world. but the union's policy is completely inconsistent, even hypocritical. you say you are committed to the principles of human rights in the world, but i do not hear you talking about the many christians, jews and women in the muslim world. they live as second-class citizens and are severely oppressed. and i don't hear you about the human rights situation in turkey, where journalists are in prison for criticizing the government or criticizing islam. and i don't hear you talking about human rights in egypt, where islamists are changing the constitution to introduce sharia, a real horror for the minorities in that country. the conclusion, mr president, is that the eu's human rights policy is utterly unbelievable, a farce.
as rapporteur for eu-china relations, i have noted paragraph 141 of the donskis report with great satisfaction. on the basis of international human rights law, this paragraph rightly criticises the repressive attitude of the chinese government towards the thriving protestant house church and the underground roman catholic church. beijing should not impose any registration requirements on this church, let alone any unlawful government attempts to deny these unregistered churches and their congregants the fundamental right to religious freedom. in short, i am very grateful that the european parliament is speaking with one voice on this urgent and cardinal issue of freedom of religion and belief. i expect the same from the council and the commission, of course, especially when i hear that the chinese house churches are already taking account of increasing government interference in the approaching christmas period.
on behalf of the ppe group i note, mrs malmström, that you have incorporated much of your past experience, but also of your current work, into your speech, and i thank you very much for that. i also thank the rapporteur, mr swoboda. we in the ppe group can fully agree with his report. i would like to say that i am very pleased that the commission has taken this initiative. russia is our biggest neighbor and you normally have a good relationship with neighbors. we have a saying in the netherlands: 'better a good neighbour than a distant friend'. but i have a feeling that russia is becoming less and less of a good neighbor. it seems that they are not interested in having a good relationship. the efta delegation next week, invited by russia, notwithstanding the fact that the russian government's counterparts are not present, is a clear example of this. president, if we as parliamentarians want to talk about strengthening mutual interests, about the rule of law, which is so important to both of us, about the rule of law, about strengthening democracy, about fully implementing civil liberties and human rights, then the russian authorities will not give in. the same applies to the strategic partnership and cooperation agreement. there are no real negotiations anymore, because russia is really only interested in visa facilitation and good agreements that are beneficial for russia in the energy field. mr swoboda and all of us want to get that file out of the partnership agreement quickly. but then we must not be left on the sidelines here as parliament, but also as the european commission. that means that the treaty should be short and should actually contain only one word and that is: i'm not sure. mr president, we have indicated in the report that what russia always says to us is: the european union is not a party to the treaty of rome, but it is a party to the treaty of rome. the way in which the opposition is silenced, the way in which the work of civil society is made more difficult, is also very bad for the economic and social development of russia itself. i would like to see a better relationship with russia, mr president, and we are now again indicating what should be done to achieve that.
just a week ago, a dutch correspondent in russia reported on the increasing difficulties in being able to carry out his profession as a journalist in a normal way. he had traveled to the north caucasus, to stavropol, and was approached there when he was watching a parade of cossacks as if he were a foreign spy, and he said that actually half of his local contacts had canceled the conversation with him there. and two hotels turned him down. that brings me to the point already raised by my dutch colleague, mrs oomen-ruijten: a strategic partnership requires reciprocity. that means free press for russian journalists outside russia in the european union, but also free press for european journalists in russia. i hope that this important point will be brought to russia by our authorities in the negotiations on the strategic partnership.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, this is another urgent appeal for leadership by vice-president/high representative ashton. early last year the eu made a promise to our citizens and to people in north africa and the middle east who called for freedom, justice, human rights and opportunities. we saw the consequences of supporting decades of dictatorial regimes and decided that, even with an uncertain future ahead, the eu needed a more values-based policy to build partnerships with people in these countries. our promise to reward more democratic reforms with more financial assistance has barely been implemented, but is now being seriously tested. president morsi made the very bad judgement of grabbing more power than president mubarak had ever had, placing himself beyond judicial scrutiny. our efforts to help bring investments and economic reconstruction to egypt are replaced by serious concern and the need for urgent crisis management. the egyptian people do not deserve this leadership – inexperienced yet power-hungry. democracy is much more than ‘one man, one vote’, and relying on checks on power via the streets is not sustainable. egypt needs the rule of law. and what has been the eu response to this proactive power grab? it took weeks for a very weak statement to be issued, and the first statement by the vice-president/high representative after 22 november 2012 was an announcement that the eu would return frozen assets of the mubarak regime to egypt. a more poorly timed and wrongly guided message is hardly imaginable. a partnership with the new egypt is very important to us. when people in our neighbourhood flourish, so do we, and this is not just an economic story. we need to stand firmly for the universal rights of all egyptian people. and it is disappointing that the eu is not stronger. we are letting the people of egypt as well as our own promises down. with regard to the referendum and any developments that may unfold we must be much more vigilant or we should stop pretending about conditionality.
what do you do when a friend starts doing something strange and you can no longer understand why he is doing it? what do you do when a country is trying to find a democracy, but it's so polarized that there's no room for compromise? we were in egypt recently with the task force, with mrs ashton, and i noticed in talks with parliamentarians in egypt that it is not in the customs or in the manners of the day to compromise, to take a step, to know when to take a step back, to leave room for the colleague who does not agree with you. and that means we are looking at a country that seems to be off track. and yes, i get incredibly worried when i see how president morsi operates. but i also know that when a friend does something crazy, you tell him the truth first: mr morsi, in three weeks to pass a constitution is very unwise, but 2. you stay with it and it stays your friend and you keep looking for ways to assist. megaphone diplomacy is not the way the european union is going to restore the rule of law in egypt. my request to mrs ashton and to mr füle is therefore: stay with it! the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
the european parliament has been very active in the debate on the draft constitution, and it is not surprising that the european parliament has been very active in the debate on the draft constitution. fortunately, she is far from alone. a close reading of the text of the draft constitution makes it clear why christians and secularists in egypt strongly oppose this text from the muslim brotherhood. some concrete examples to illustrate: article 219 allows for the codification and full application of the sharia, islamic law, and as appears from article 4, promotes the high council of clerics of al-azhar as the sole competent authority to interpret laws and to check whether they are in accordance with the constitution and the sharia. this constitutional article aims at a decisive influence of islamic scholars, the ulema, on the judicial, legislative and executive branches of government. no less than that! what conclusions and consequences do the council and the commission draw from this scenario of president morsi or, probably much earlier, of the 'murshid', the real leader of egypt, mohammed badai, the head of the muslim brotherhood?
mr president, much has been said today, but let us be honest. from the day it came into existence, the state of israel has been confronted with terror and war – a war israel did not seek itself. today, hamas in the south and in the gaza strip, and hezbollah in the north, are engaging in terrorism. that is bad for the palestinians, but what can israel do but react? i do not believe that extra settlements are the right reaction. what we, the european parliament, should do is tell hamas to stop this terror now, to recognise israel and to sit at a table together with their palestinian authority. we should tell them to stop now.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i think that many in this house are united. we believe in bringing ukraine and the european union closer together. we are committed, just as the commission is committed. commissioner füle has our full support. as we look at the benchmarks, the osce and the odihr have concluded that the elections were a setback. that is a very serious thing. not progress, but a setback. and how can this be changed? ukraine must act and must act by making an electoral law which is up to european or international standards. it should do so. if we look at the referendum law, that should also be brought up to the standards set out by the commission. and look at law 8711, under which people who speak out on behalf of homosexuals and others may be prosecuted. this law should either be repealed by the government or should be voted down by the rada. there is much to be done, and ukraine should take the european union seriously. we reach out our hands, and i think this is true for the whole house here, the commission and the council. it is now up to ukraine to make a difference, and i agree with both previous speakers.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, concerning albania, one of our greatest worries is always the political climate, the lack of even the willingness to cooperate between the different main parties. things seem to have improved in the last year, and for a while their parliament seemed to be in working order. but now i am not so sure. in november, three requested pieces of legislation failed to pass in the parliament as a result of a totally unrelated power struggle. why? prime minister berisha announced that all ethnic albanians could get albanian passports, knowing full well what this would cause in the region. he has been a politician for far too long not to realise this. why? our parliamentary delegation meeting, which was planned for next week, was cancelled at the last moment. i had my suitcase ready. i was looking forward to constructive and cooperative dialogue with the coalition and opposition, but they obviously were not. why? all of that came just before a very important council meeting. it was basically a concerted effort to ruin all chances of albania gaining candidate status. why on earth? to me it seems that the campaign for next year’s election has started in earnest already, this early. disregarding what the country needs, disregarding the european agenda. i for one would love to grant the people of albania candidate status, but i am afraid that their chosen politicians have again lost themselves in an internal power struggle, and that that will not be possible until they get their priorities straight again. so please, please, mr berisha, mr rama, in the interests of your people, please. thank you.
i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the council has not yet taken a decision on the matter. i can talk to the commissioner, but at the end of the day, this is about the willingness of the member states to take in refugees. i hear you say that the european union is prepared to pay for shelter in the region. but we know the numbers now: half a million syrians have fled to neighboring countries. when we put a question on this subject, some 300 syrians had entered europe and i do not know how many syrians have now boarded a boat in turkey to reach a greek island and have not made it. we can be loyal, and we can not send back syrians if they manage to reach the european borders, we can offer them temporary protection, but what remains is that we don't actually take syrians in. in fact, we are making it difficult for turkey, we are reprimanding turkey at the moment that they are allowing syrians to cross to reach europe. my colleague papanikolaou has already made it clear that if we do not tackle this problem together, a number of countries in europe will again be in serious trouble, including greece, of course. my comment to the council is therefore: stop saying we will pay for shelter in the region, stop predicting that it will be a trend for refugees from syria to come to europe, but make sure that we, with 27 member states, start taking refugees and taking them out of jordan and turkey, because they will not survive the winter well.
on behalf of the green group, i would like to thank mrs tatjana ždanoka, who is faced with an incredibly complex strategic decision. let me take you back to this week: on monday mr van rompuy received the nobel peace prize together with mr schulz and mr barroso, on tuesday evening we debated the state of human rights in the european union in 2010 and 2011, on wednesday morning we had a ceremony here to celebrate the european union's receipt of the nobel peace prize, on wednesday afternoon we had the representatives of the sakharov prize winners, on wednesday evening we were allowed to speak about the human rights situation in the rest of the world, and today we are here to talk about the work of the fundamental rights office. we talked about human rights all week, sometimes it was a pleasure, but sometimes it was a cry. that's it! how is it possible that we can point the finger and be very sharp when it comes to human rights in the world, but when it comes to human rights in europe, the council says that we can do a little less. i would like to quote mr schulz, who said in the plenary session here on wednesday morning: "peace, to me, begins with the absence of the word 'but.' democracy is good, "but" ..., human rights must be guaranteed, "but" ..., tolerance is good, "but" ..., and he continues and ends with "that can't be". i think we should get rid of the word 'but', we should respect each other's human rights, without the word 'but'. there is such a big "but" to the fundamental rights office and its work for the coming year. they're not supposed to do anything they're not asked to do, but there's one crucial thing missing from their job description, and that's judicial cooperation. member states do not want any snooping in their criminal investigations and in their way of storing and exchanging data. i find that very disturbing, and you see what comes of it; i am thinking of the european arrest warrant, where citizens are transferred from one country to another, trapped in sometimes terrible conditions in pre-trial detention that can last for incredibly long periods of time; then they are brought before a judge, perhaps for a crime too small to be so exterminated. judicial cooperation should really be part of the investigative capacity of the fundamental rights office. and until that happens, we're basically looking away and saying, "yes, but the member states and their criminal cases are all right" or "we actually think that human rights are a little less applicable there". i think this parliament should keep its back straight. it is wise that we agree now. we are keeping our backs up, but i would like to remind you that the debate we have had this week on the state of human rights in europe has taken on a political dimension in recent years. i wonder if that's the right way.
we are discussing today the work programme of the european union agency for fundamental rights. the agency shall provide assistance and expertise in the field of fundamental rights to the institutions of the union and to the member states. but to be a member of the union, a state must already have stable institutions, and these must provide guarantees in the field of democracy, the rule of law, human rights, respect for minorities and their protection. the member states have therefore known these fundamental rights for a long time, otherwise they would not be members of the union. that makes this office completely redundant. the bureau is used mainly as a symbol of european morality; we can show the rest of the world how high we stand on human rights, how greatly we do on this old continent. but the advertising column has to have something to do, and that's why the agency came up with the plan to make free health care for illegal immigrants a duty of the member states. new fundamental rights are being invented all the time. mr president, real fundamental rights are the right of association, the right of the press, the right to freedom of expression, but this european parliament is not in favour of that at all. our president, mr schulz, condemned the distribution of a film here the other day. fundamental rights are not taken seriously here and are used only as political toys. the bureau exists only for the greater glory of the moral knights in the european parliament and therefore i think that the bureau should be abolished. it saves money and annoyance.
president. - mr commissioner, we have been talking about growth in europe for some time now and it has become a magic word in politics. we need growth and more jobs. but i think it has to do with consumer and business confidence in the future of the market. the government is not an entrepreneur, and fortunately so. we create conditions that enable businesses and consumers to conduct their business and make purchases in a familiar environment. the government provides those conditions, and what does this show? many of the laws that are being made in europe are seen as hindering entrepreneurship. why would you do that? because, as is often said, we say so, but we have never put it into practice, because we are still working with directives that are transposed incorrectly or differently in each country, which means that there are enormous differences in the internal market. i have been advocating for years to focus on regulations that work equally everywhere. and for the rest - if we are to work with directives per se - let us renationalize and return the cases to the member states. let's enter that separately. that means a huge saving of work in europe and we are left with only european legislation that works everywhere and is the same everywhere for every consumer and every entrepreneur. when investing, think of tax benefits instead of subsidies! because subsidies, it turns out, work like a sleeping pill, and a sleeping pill makes companies sleepy and not actually working on innovation. so let us call innovation 'innovation' and create the jobs we all want.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, commissioner, colleagues, a toxic cocktail of violence over natural resources, rape, the recruiting and employment of child soldiers, and poverty are destabilising eastern congo and affecting almost two and a half million people. paramilitary groups are continuing to commit the worst crimes and are even helped by neighbours. it is clear that the eu needs to act in a coordinated manner with the united nations and the african union to push for an end to these ongoing and horrible events, and also to prevent an even further regional spill-over in the entire great lakes region. the eu should act jointly and strongly. now several eu member states have suspended aid to rwanda as a result of the un report that alleges involvement in the conflicts in the democratic republic of the congo. we also need an eu position towards all the individuals who violated the arms embargo. we can do more on the demand side by improving transparency in reporting on illegally mined minerals, for example. independent investigations are also needed. the perpetrators of human rights violations, war crimes, and crimes against humanity, sexual violence against women, and the recruiting of child soldiers should be brought to justice. the impunity must end, and i want to draw specific attention to the cowardly attack on the courageous dr mukwege, which killed his bodyguard. this case still needs to be independently investigated. he helped many women, and it is women who are the main victims of the ongoing violence.
- a writer. - it is right that the european parliament is holding a plenary debate today on caste discrimination in india. because the 200 million dalits, the uncastrated, are in a situation that is often hopeless. they have a terrible job to do and i will not go into too much detail about that now, just after lunch. sadly, many also end up in forced prostitution. the indian government has been promising for years that it will tackle the problems and on paper the plans and rules that are being devised look pretty good too, but the day-to-day practice is completely different. one of the biggest problems is that the people who destroyed the dalit's homes, sold them as slaves and forced them into prostitution are not being prosecuted. in fact, each year hundreds, possibly thousands, of dalits are tortured by the police. actually, the dalits in india are bird-free. mr president, that must change and that can change. i believe that we as the european union can make a significant contribution to this. we are negotiating with india on energy and our trade relations. i think we should not forget the position of the unfortunate. india must reform the police and judicial apparatus in such a way that the poorest and most discriminated against are not pushed further down but are protected. let that be a condition of the european union in its relations with india and in the talks on the new trade agreement.
author. − mr president, as caste discrimination and modern-day slavery are still serious and systematic problems affecting hundreds of millions of indians, more needs to be done in terms of the efforts that the authorities have taken, and more can be done than has been so far. we hope that the indian parliament will pass a proposed bill which would create more protection for the most vulnerable people and minority groups. there also needs to be improvement in both the reporting of cases of discrimination and abuse and the security of the people who report it, and the people responsible for such abuse need to be held accountable. ngos or civil society groups that support the most vulnerable people in india should not be hindered from receiving funds from third countries. the eu should step up its efforts to address these problems locally in india and reach out to these minority populations. however, we should also address discrimination and abuse more firmly in the course of international dialogues or eu-india dialogues. as the world watches with interest how india is going to develop economically, we should be equally firm in ensuring that its progress and development are reflected in the improvement of human rights protection as well.
